The master equation for mean field game systems with fractional and nonlocal diffusions
Espen R. Jakobsen, Artur Rutkowski

Abstract
. We prove existence and uniqueness of classical solutions of the master equation for mean field game (MFG) systems with fractional and nonlocal diffusions. We cover a large class of Lévy diffusions of order greater than one, including purely nonlocal, local, and even mixed local-nonlocal operators. In the process we prove refined well-posedness results for the MFG systems, results that include the mixed local-nonlocal case. We also show various auxiliary results on viscous Hamilton-Jacobi equations, linear parabolic equations, and linear forward-backward systems that may be of independent interest. This includes a rigorous treatment of certain equations and systems with data and solutions in the duals of Hölder spaces C b γ C^{\gamma}_{b} on the whole of ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . We do not assume existence of any moments for the initial distributions of players. In a future work we will use the results of this paper to prove the convergence of N N -player games to mean field games as N → ∞ N\to\infty .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q89, 35S10, 35A01, 35A08, 35K08, 49L12, 45K05, 35K61, 46E15, 46G05

1.
Introduction

The goal of this paper is to show well-posedness for the master equation associated to the mean field game (MFG) system

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u+H(x,u,Du)=F(x,m(t))\quad&\textrm{in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\partial_{t}m-\mathcal{L}^{*}m-\Div(mD_{p}H(x,u,Du))=0\quad&\textrm{in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
m(t_{0})=m_{0},\qquad u(T,x)=G(x,m(T)).\end{cases}

Here
\mathcal{L}
is a nonlocal or mixed local-nonlocal (or even just local) diffusion operator, a constant coefficient Lévy operator defined for
u\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

(1.2)

\mathcal{L}u(x)=B\cdot Du(x)+\Div(ADu(x))+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+z)-u(x)-Du(x)\cdot z\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(z))\,\nu(dz),

with Lévy triplet
(B,A,\nu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where

A

is a positive semi-definite symmetric
d\times d
matrix,
and
\nu
is a nonnegative Borel measure satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1\wedge|z|^{2})\nu(dz)<\infty
, called the Lévy measure. By
\mathcal{L}^{*}
we denote the formal adjoint of
\mathcal{L}
, cf. Subsection
2.4
. The system describes a Nash equilibrium of a mean field game, a limit of
N
-player games as
N\to\infty
under suitable assumptions. We refer to
[
34
]
for a heuristic derivation of (
1.1
) from the related control problem. The first equation of (
1.1
) is a backward in time viscous Hamilton–Jacobi (H–J) equation for the value function
u
of a generic player. The second equation is a forward in time Fokker–Planck (F–P) equation for
the distribution of players
m
. The equations are nonlinear and coupled. The coupling terms
F
and
G
are nonlocal in the sense that they depend only on
m(t)
instead of
m(t,x)
. The operator
\mathcal{L}
corresponds to the so-called individual/idiosyncratic noise, we do not study cases with common noise in this paper.

MFG systems were first introduced by Lasry and Lions
[
53
]
and Huang, Malhamé, and Caines
[
44
]
as a way to study games with a large number of players. Since then, the topic grew immensely and found its applications, e.g., in finance, economy, energy management, crowd and traffic modelling, and mathematical biology. There are two main approaches to MFGs: probabilistic
[
44
,
16
,
17
]
and PDE
[
53
,
2
]
. This paper is written within the PDE setting. We refer to the books
[
2
,
13
,
16
,
17
,
39
]
for derivations of the models, discussion of the different approaches,
and further reading.
MFGs with non-Gaussian noise and corresponding nonlocal diffusion operators
\mathcal{L}
are still a rather new research direction with relatively few works, especially compared to MFGs with Gaussian noise/local operators. Cesaroni et al.
[
20
]
studied stationary (time-independent) systems on the torus driven by the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
. In
[
24
]
Cirant and Goffi work with time-dependent equations, allowing all
\alpha\in(0,2)
, but the supercritical exponents
\alpha\in(0,1]
required an entirely different approach and weaker type of solutions. Recently, Ye, Zou, and Liu
[
70
]
gave a well-posedness result for subcritical fractional time-dependent MFG systems on torus, with non-separable Hamiltonians. Some mixed local-nonlocal diffusions were studied in the context of Bertrand and Cournot MFGs by Graber, Ignazio, and Neufeld
[
40
]
. Benazzoli, Campi, and Di Persio
[
7
]
have studied mean field games from the probabilistic point of view for diffusions with jumps of finite intensity. We also mention here the recent work of Cavallazzi
[
18
]
on the propagation of chaos for stable driven McKean–Vlasov SDEs.
One of the key references for our work is
[
34
]
by Ersland and Jakobsen. The paper provides well-posedness for the MFG systems in the whole Euclidean space, driven by more general nonlocal operators of order greater than
1
, i.e. nondegenerate operators with a sufficiently strong regularizing effect to produce smooth solutions of the
u
-equation in (
1.1
).
The assumption on the order of the operator was given in
[
34
]
in terms of the heat kernel. We use a slightly weaker version, see
(K)
below. It allows very general operators, e.g.,
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
with
\alpha\in(1,2]
, mixed local-nonlocal operators, strongly anisotropic stable operators like
(-\partial^{2}_{x_{1}})^{\alpha_{1}/2}+(-\partial^{2}_{x_{2}})^{\alpha_{2}/2}+\ldots+(-\partial^{2}_{x_{d}})^{\alpha_{d}/2}
, tempered operators used in finance like the CGMY model, strongly non-symmetric operators, and operators generating Lévy processes with no finite moments,
see Section
2.6
below. While most analytic results on MFGs are formulated on bounded domains (but see e.g.
[
36
]
), this paper considers the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
without any moment conditions on
m
.

Note that it is important for us to work without moment conditions to cover important classes of purely nonlocal operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The moments of
m
are determined by the moments of the initial measure
m_{0}
and the driving noise generated by
\mathcal{L}
, where the latter is determined by the moments of
\nu
at infinity
[
67
]
. As opposed to the local case with Gaussian noise having finite moments of all orders, processes generated by nonlocal operators may have infinite moments. A canonical example is
the isotropic
\alpha
-stable process generated by the fractional Laplacian
\mathcal{L}=-(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
, which has infinite moments of all orders
\geq\alpha
.

By a solution to the MFG system (
1.1
) we mean a pair
(u,m)
such that
u
solves the Hamilton–Jacobi equation in the classical sense and
m\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
solves the Fokker–Planck equation in the distributional sense. Because we do not assume moments, we work in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the space of probability measures, endowed with the Kantorovich–Rubinstein (or bounded-Lipschitz) metric
d_{0}
, see Section
2.2
.
The existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
) is given in Theorem
4.3
. Despite the similar setting this is a different result than the one in
[
34
]
, where the authors pursued a unified approach for local and nonlocal couplings, which produced two artifacts: the need to consider smooth solutions in the Fokker–Planck equation and the restriction to purely nonlocal operators. Still, many of the results and arguments of
[
34
]
are applicable in our work. We obtain slightly stronger Hölder estimates for the Hamilton–Jacobi equation than in
[
34
]
by using in full the regularity given by the heat kernel. Similar to
[
34
]
we do not assume any moment conditions on
m_{0}
or
\nu
at infinity. Instead we use the fact that probability measures
m_{0}
and
\nu|_{B(0,1)^{c}}
(properly normalized) both have a certain generalized moment.
We then show that this moment is
uniformly bounded for the solution
m(t)
of the Fokker–Planck equation and hence get tightness of
\{m(t):t\in[t_{0},T]\}
, a property crucial for compactness arguments. The preservation of the generalized moment for MFG systems in the whole space was observed by Chowdhury, Jakobsen, and Krupski
[
23
]
. Note that this is not an issue on the torus or other bounded domains, as every probability measure there has all the moments. However, other papers on the whole space adopt some moment assumptions due to using Wasserstein
d_{1}
or
d_{2}
distances, or arguments from probability and stochastic calculus, which usually require second moments. Note that the probabilistic approach based on a McKean–Vlasov FBSDE reformulation of the MFG,
has been quite successful in dealing with problems on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see e.g.
[
15
]
or
[
16
, Chapter 3]
and references therein.

Our Hamiltonian is separable
of the form
H(x,Du)-F(x,m)
. Models with more general non-separable Hamiltonians
\widetilde{H}(x,Du,m)
have been considered in
e.g.
[
4
,
36
,
37
,
58
,
60
,
70
]
.
On the other hand, our existence and uniqueness result for the MFG system allows
u
in the Hamiltonian, which to our knowledge is new. The main obstacle in this case is the uniqueness – with
u
-dependent Hamiltonian it requires a modified version of the Lasry–Lions monotonicity argument, which we give in Lemma
4.2
. Although it is sufficient for uniqueness for the MFG system, Lemma
4.2
does not seem strong enough to get stability results needed for the master equation. Thus, in the further results we switch to Hamiltonians depending only on
x
and
Du
.

The main result of this work, Theorem
6.2
, gives the existence and uniqueness for the master equation associated with the MFG system (
1.1
):

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}U(t,x,m)\hskip-10.0pt&=-\mathcal{L}_{x}U(t,x,m)+H(x,D_{x}U(t,x,m))-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t,x,m,y)\,m(dy)-F(x,m)\\
&\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t,x,m,y)D_{p}H(y,D_{y}U(t,y,m))\,m(dy)\quad\mathrm{in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
U(T,x,m)\hskip-10.0pt&=G(x,m)\quad\mathrm{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\end{cases}

The equation is set in the infinite dimensional space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of probability measures. The expression
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
is the derivative in this space; it is somewhat similar to the Gateaux/Fréchet derivative, but here we do not have a linear structure, see Section
2.3
for the definition and properties of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
. The master equation contains full information about the MFG systems with different initial times
t_{0}
and measures
m_{0}
.
To be precise, for any point
(t_{0},x)
and measure
m_{0}
, the solution
U
of the master equation is given as

(1.4)

\displaystyle U(t_{0},x,m_{0}):=u(t_{0},x)

where
(u,m)
solves (
1.1
) on
[t_{0},T]
with initial measure
m_{0}
. The function
U
is sometimes called the master field, or in analogy with the theory of FBSDEs, the decoupling field
[
2
,
17
]
.

The master equation was introduced by P.-L. Lions in his lectures at Collège de France, and the first result about its well-posedness was given by Chassagneux, Crisan and Delarue
[
21
]
. The key reference for us is the seminal work of Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions
[
13
]
, in which the master equation (
1.3
) is studied from a PDE/analytical point of view.
The purpose of the master equation is to provide a way of quantifying how Nash equilibria in finite-player games converge to Nash equilibria in mean field games,
i.e., it facilitates solving the so-called convergence problem. In recent years there have been several works in this direction, see e.g.
[
13
,
11
,
17
,
28
,
65
]
. We also expect this approach to work in the nonlocal case, and we will address this problem in a future work using results of the current paper.

Aside from
[
13
, Chapter 3]
, the most relevant references using the deterministic setting, are the works on bounded domains, by Ricciardi
[
64
,
65
]
for the Neumann condition and by Di Persio, Garbelli, and Ricciardi
[
28
]
for the Dirichlet condition. Here we also mention Graber and Sircar
[
41
]
who investigate the master equation for Cournot MFGs on the half-line, Ambrose and Mészáros
[
4
]
who consider non-separable Hamiltonians and the Sobolev space setting, and Cecchin and Pelino
[
19
]
who work on the whole space with initial measures being a finite sum of Dirac deltas. Recently, Bansil and Mészáros
[
6
]
have given a framework for obtaining well-posedness for the master equation by appropriately shifting the Hamiltonian.
There are also studies of the master equation in numerous different settings, e.g., with common noise
[
13
,
14
,
26
,
25
,
37
]
, on finite state spaces
[
8
,
25
]
, with a major player
[
12
,
54
]
, and many more.

Our work is the first study of master equations with nonlocal diffusion operators. Our results also include the case of mixed local-nonlocal operators. For the MFG systems in general, along with
[
40
]
, it is the first
to study mixed local-nonlocal operators. Whereas
[
40
]
considers one-dimensional diffusions and the nonlocal part is treated as a lower order perturbation, our setting is much more general and works without any dominating local diffusion. It is also the first paper to study local MFG systems on the whole space without any moment conditions on the initial distributions of players.

On the structural level, the proof of Theorem
6.2
follows the program of
[
13
, Chapter 3]
. The majority of the work goes into establishing the existence and regularity of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
for
U
defined in (
1.4
). The first step is the result on the stability with respect to
m_{0}
for the MFG system in Theorem
5.3
, which yields Lipschitz continuity in
m
for
U
. Then, in order to access and study
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
, we linearize the MFG system (
1.1
), by subtracting two systems with different initial measures
m_{0}
. This motivates the generic linear system result in Theorem
5.5
. Taking derivatives in the ambient variable
y
(e.g.,
D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
) corresponds to differentiating the initial condition in the linearized system, and therefore we need to allow some of the data and solutions in Theorem
5.5
to belong to negative order Hölder spaces (like distributional derivatives of Dirac measures). This is a major technical issue producing strong assumptions, as this lack of regularity needs to be compensated by higher regularity of the other coefficients in the system. Ricciardi
[
64
]
proposed an improvement in this regard, exploiting the fact that some of the coefficients need not be regular uniformly in time. For the short-time existence, Cardaliaguet, Cirant, and Porretta
[
12
]
managed to separate the regularity of the coefficients in the linearized system, but because of the forward-backward nature of the problem, their results cannot be applied to arbitrarily long (but finite) time intervals that we study here. We note that while working in the whole space we need to handle many technical issues arising from the fact that the duals of Hölder spaces are much more complicated on unbounded sets. For example, there are functionals with support “at infinity” like the Banach limits, see Definition
2.2
. Furthermore, a new compactness argument is needed in the proof of Theorem
5.5
, because in the whole space the Hölder spaces are not embedded in their duals and the Arzelà–Ascoli approach used in previous works can no longer be used. To overcome this we work in an
L^{1}
-setting and use the Kolmogorov–Riesz theorem.

We note that the probabilistic approach has been effective for solving master equations with local diffusions in the whole space
[
17
,
21
]
.
By a lifting procedure

D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
is obtained directly as a Fréchet derivative and
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
is no longer needed in the analysis.
Stochastic calculus then seems to allow for data of lower regularity than we use here, see
[
17
, Section 5.1.5]
. Some significant
developments of this theory are likely still needed to cover the case of jump diffusions and diffusions without moments.

The results used for well-posedness of the master equation involve viscous Hamilton–Jacobi and linear parabolic problems in many different settings. In the case of
\mathcal{L}=\Delta
and other local operators, there is a well-established extensive literature, e.g.,
[
52
,
55
]
. Regularity for parabolic equations with nonlocal operators was also intensively studied over the last decades, see e.g.,
[
22
,
30
,
31
,
46
,
59
]
, but it is much more difficult to find the desired theorems under the right assumptions. Therefore, in this paper we formulate and prove several results for single equations: apart from the classical solution setting (Lemma
3.3
, Theorem
3.10
), which was largely present in
[
34
]
, we also discuss the
L^{1}
setting (Lemma
3.5
), the probability measure setting (Theorem
3.9
), and the setting of the negative order Hölder spaces (Lemma
5.7
). These results are quite
general and could be of independent interest.

Due to the excessive amount of technical details and the complicated structure of the proof, we found that some results in the previous papers require modifications of the proofs or formulations. The details and references are given in Section
2.7
. Of course, this does not affect the high value of the original and sometimes groundbreaking ideas which these works contributed.
In the work of Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry, and Lions
[
13
]
, which is a basis for many further papers (including this one), the necessary amendments are limited to using slightly stronger assumptions and correcting some of the intermediate results by weakening their conclusions. We made a substantial effort to make the arguments rigorous while keeping the assumptions fairly mild.

The paper is organized as follows: in Section
2.1
we discuss the Hölder spaces, Section
2.2
is devoted to the Kantorovich–Rubinstein metric, in Section
2.3
we introduce the measure derivatives, and in Section
2.4
the operator
\mathcal{L}
and its heat kernel. Assumptions are formulated in Section
2.5
and examples are given in Section
2.6
. In Section
2.7
we discuss the previous mistakes. Section
3
contains the results about the single equations. In Section
4
we prove well-posedness of the MFG system. Section
5
gives further regularity for the MFG system, including the existence of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
. Finally, in Section
6
we prove the main result – well-posedness of classical solution of the master equation with nonlocal diffusion.

1.1.
Summary of the main results and contributions.

•

Well-posedness of the master equation – Theorem
6.2
:

–

First such result for nonlocal diffusions.

–

Allowing mixed local-nonlocal operators.

–

Allowing local diffusion operators in the whole space without moment conditions.

–

Rigorous treatment of duals of Hölder spaces in an unbounded domain, new also for the Laplacian.

•

Well-posedness of the MFG system – Theorem
4.3
.

–

First such result for mixed local-nonlocal operators.

–

No moment conditions imposed for
m_{0}
in the case of local diffusions.

–

More optimal Schauder estimates and general (non-smooth)
m_{0}
compared to
[
34
]
.

–

Hamiltonians depending on
u
.

•

Auxiliary results for Hamilton–Jacobi equations and linear equations with divergence and non-divergence form drift with quite general Lévy diffusions – Lemma
3.3
, Lemma
3.5
, Theorem
3.9
, Theorem
3.10
, Lemma
5.7
.

•

Observing and fixing some oversights in previous work – Section
2.7
.

2.
Preliminaries

Constants (usually denoted by
c
or
C
) may change value from line to line. We define the family of cut-off functions
\chi_{R}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,1]
for
R\geq 1
, as functions satisfying:
\chi_{R}=1
on
B(0,R)
,
\chi_{R}=0
on
B(0,R+1)^{c}
, and
\|\chi_{R}\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C
, with
C
independent of
R
.
In several results we use approximation by convolution with a standard mollifier
\eta
, that is, a non-negative radially decreasing smooth function supported in the unit ball such that
\int\eta=1
. For
\varepsilon>0
we denote
\eta_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-d}\eta(x/\varepsilon)
. Note that
\|D\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
and
\|D^{2}\eta_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon^{-2}
.
Spaces
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
p\in[1,\infty]
are the usual spaces of equivalence classes of measurable functions with the
p
-th power integrable (or essentially bounded for
p=\infty
).

2.1.
Hölder and related function spaces

Remark 2.1
.

This subsection is quite long and might be skipped on the first reading. We explain the intricacies of the duals of Hölder spaces on unbounded domains. Furthermore, we address in detail the issue of passing to the limit inside
C^{\alpha}
–
(C^{\alpha})^{*}
pairings, and for this sake we define iterated spaces, see Lemma
2.8
and Remark
2.9
below.

By
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we denote the space of bounded continuous real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, then
C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of functions with continuous and bounded derivatives up to the order
k
(in particular
C^{0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
). The norm is given by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sum\limits_{|\beta|\leq k}\|\partial^{\beta}u\|_{\infty}.

We denote by
C^{k}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of functions in
C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose derivatives up to order
k
vanish at infinity. The space
C^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consists of compactly supported functions in
C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,1)
. We define the Hölder seminorm of a function
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle|u|_{C^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sup\limits_{x\neq y}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\sigma}}.

The Hölder space of order
k+\sigma
for
k\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
is

\displaystyle C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\big\{u\in C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):|\partial^{\beta}u|_{C^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\ {\rm for\ all}\ |\beta|=k\big\}.

Here, the norm is given by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|u\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sum\limits_{|\beta|=k}|\partial^{\beta}u|_{C^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

It is well-known that Hölder spaces are Banach spaces with the above norm.

The negative order Hölder spaces are defined as the duals, i.e., for
\gamma\geq 0

we let
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=(C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{*}
and

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sup\limits_{\|\phi\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1}\frac{\langle\rho,\phi\rangle}{\|\phi\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}.

Note that
C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\neq C^{0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Some of the
\rho\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be identified with an integrable function. Indeed, if we let
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

(2.1)

\displaystyle\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)f(x)\,dx,

then
\rho\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
\gamma\geq 0
and
\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\gamma,d)\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. This gives an embedding of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Similar embedding holds for the space of finite signed Borel measures
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the total variation norm
\|\cdot\|_{TV}
. Furthermore, if
0\leq\gamma_{1}\leq\gamma_{2}
and
\rho\in C^{-\gamma_{1}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then we have

\displaystyle\|\rho|_{C^{\gamma_{2}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{C^{-\gamma_{2}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma_{1}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

although this does not yield an
embedding
between the spaces, because the restriction map is not injective. Note that in contrast with bounded domains, on the whole space we do not have the natural embedding of
C_{b}
into
C^{-0}_{b}
, because the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}fg
for
f,g\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
may not exist.

Contrary to the spaces over compact sets, not all functionals in
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be represented using integrals against (countably additive) measures. For example, the space
C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be identified with
finitely
additive set functions, but they are much more difficult to work with, see Dunford and Schwartz
[
33
, Theorem IV.6.1.2]
. Note that the space
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
does not fall into any family of distributions, because the functionals may depend on the behavior of function at infinity.

Definition 2.2
.

We call
L\in C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
a Banach limit, if
L(f)=\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)
whenever the limit exists.

The existence of Banach limits follows from the Hahn–Banach theorem. The restriction of a Banach limit to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is the zero distribution. Furthermore, a Banach limit cannot be represented as integration against a signed measure.

If
f\in C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then we let

\langle\rho f,\phi\rangle=\langle\rho,f\phi\rangle
and we have

\displaystyle\|\rho f\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\gamma,d)\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

For smooth
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with all derivatives integrable we let
\overline{f}(x)=f(-x)
and we define

\displaystyle\langle\rho\ast f,\phi\rangle=\langle\rho,\phi\ast\overline{f}\rangle,

for all
\phi
for which the right-hand side makes sense.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\rho\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\gamma\geq 0
and assume that
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
f,Df,D^{2}f,\ldots\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\rho\ast f\in C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, for
f=\eta_{\varepsilon}
we have
\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\rho
in
C^{-\gamma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
\delta>0
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\displaystyle|\langle\rho\ast f,\phi\rangle|=|\langle\rho,\phi\ast\overline{f}\rangle|\leq\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi\ast\overline{f}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi\ast\overline{f}\|_{C^{\lceil\gamma\rceil}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi\|_{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\lceil\gamma\rceil}\|D^{k}f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Dividing by
\|\phi\|_{\infty}
implies that
\rho\ast f\in C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For the second part of the statement let
\phi\in C^{\gamma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then,

\displaystyle|\langle\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}-\rho,\phi\rangle|=|\langle\rho,\phi\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}-\phi\rangle|\leq\|\phi\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}-\phi\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{\delta}\|\phi\|_{C^{\gamma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

with
C
independent of
\phi
and
\varepsilon
. It follows that
\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\to\rho
in
C^{-\gamma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

In the above result we cannot infer that
\rho\ast f
can be represented as integration against a measure. For example if
L
is a Banach limit, then it is easy to see that
\langle L\ast\eta_{\varepsilon},\phi\rangle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi(x)
if the limit exists. Hence,
L\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}
is also a Banach limit.

The existence of functionals like the Banach limit motivates the need to consider a subclass of
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which can be represented as integrals with respect to countably additive measures.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
. We say that
\rho\in C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is measure representable if there exist
\mu_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
|\alpha|\leq n
, such that for any
\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi(x)\,\mu_{\alpha}(dx).

We also let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{\rho\in C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\rho\ \textrm{is measure representable}\}.

The representation in (
2.2
) is in general not unique.

Lemma 2.6
.

The class
\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a norm-closed subset of
C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consequently,
(\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
is a Banach space. Furthermore, every
\rho\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has a representation such that
\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\|\mu_{\alpha}\|_{TV}.

Note that in Lemma
2.6
we do not make a claim about uniqueness of the representation. The proof is postponed to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\rho\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
and assume that
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
f,Df,D^{2}f,\ldots\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\rho\ast f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the sense of (
2.1
) and
\|\rho\ast f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be represented as (
2.2
) with
\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\|\mu_{\alpha}\|_{TV}
, see Lemma
2.6
. Then for every
\phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\langle\rho\ast f,\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}(\phi\ast f)(x)\,\mu_{\alpha}(dx)=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi\ast\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\,\mu_{\alpha}(dx)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}(\overline{\partial^{\alpha}f}\ast\mu_{\alpha})(dx).

By assumptions on
f
, the last measure is absolutely continuous with a smooth integrable density. Furthermore, by the dual characterization of the
L^{1}
norm and the fact that
\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\|\mu_{\alpha}\|_{TV}
, we get

\displaystyle\|\rho\ast f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg\|\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}(\overline{\partial^{\alpha}f}\ast\mu_{\alpha})(dx)\bigg\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\|\overline{\partial^{\alpha}f}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\mu_{\alpha}\|_{TV}\leq C\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

∎

We will use various types of differentiable/Hölder functions with two spatial or space-time variables. First, following
[
13
]
, for
m,n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
, let
C^{m,n}_{b,x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be the space of functions
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that for all
0\leq k\leq m
,
0\leq l\leq n
the derivatives
D^{k}_{x}D^{l}_{y}u
exist and are bounded and continuous, with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{m,n}_{b,x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{l=0}^{n}\|D^{k}_{x}D^{l}_{y}u\|_{\infty}.

We also consider the space
C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for
m,n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
and
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
, endowed with the norm

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}=\|u\|_{C^{m,n}_{b,x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m}\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|D_{x}^{k}u(x,\cdot)\|_{C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{h\neq 0}\bigg\|\frac{D^{m}_{x}u(x+h,\cdot)-D^{m}_{x}u(x,\cdot)}{|h|^{\alpha}}\bigg\|_{C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In this space we can differentiate under functionals of class
(C^{-n-\beta}_{b})_{y}
with respect to the parameter
x
.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
f\in C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
m,n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta\in[0,1)
, then for any
\phi\in C^{-n-\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the map
x\mapsto\phi(f(x,\cdot))
belongs to
C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|\phi(f)\|_{C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{-n-\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))},

and we have
D^{k}_{x}\phi(f(x,\cdot))=\phi(D^{k}_{x}f(x,\cdot))
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

Proof.

First, note that for
1\leq k\leq m-1
,
1\leq i\leq d
, and
h\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{D^{k-1}_{x}f(x+he_{i},\cdot)-D^{k-1}_{x}f(x,\cdot)}{h}-\partial_{x_{i}}D^{k-1}_{x}f(x,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\int_{0}^{1}(D^{k}_{x}f(x+\lambda he_{i},\cdot)-D^{k}_{x}f(x,\cdot))\,d\lambda\bigg\|_{C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C|h|.

For
k=m
we get a similar estimate with
|h|^{\alpha}
on the right-hand side.
Therefore, since
\phi\in C^{-n-\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}D^{k-1}_{x}\phi(f(x,\cdot))=\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\phi\bigg(\frac{D^{k-1}_{x}f(x+he_{i},\cdot)-D^{k-1}_{x}f(x,\cdot)}{h}\bigg)=\phi(\partial_{x_{i}}D^{k-1}_{x}f(x,\cdot)),\quad 1\leq k\leq m,

where for
k=m
we used the fact that
\alpha>0
. The fact that
x\mapsto\phi(f(x,\cdot))
belongs to
C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with (
2.4
) now follows immediately from the definition of
\|f\|_{C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

In
[
13
, p. 29]
two variable Hölder spaces
C^{m+\alpha,n+\beta}
are defined in a similar way as our
C^{m,n}_{b,x,y}
-spaces by adding the following assumption on the highest order derivatives
D^{m}_{x}D^{n}_{y}
:

(2.5)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{(x_{1},y_{1})\neq(x_{2},y_{2})}\frac{|D^{m}_{x}D^{n}_{y}u(x_{1},y_{1})-D^{m}_{x}D^{n}_{y}u(x_{2},y_{2})|}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}+|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{\beta}}<\infty.

In this space the highest order
y
-Hölder norms are bounded, but not necessarily continuous in
x
. This is insufficient for the proof of Lemma
2.8
and one needs to consider
C^{m+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{n+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
instead.

For
T_{1}<T_{2}
and
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
we let
C^{m,2m}_{b}([T_{1},T_{2}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the usual parabolic space
[
52
]
of smooth functions with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{m,2m}_{b}([T_{1},T_{2}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq m\\
0\leq l\leq 2m\\
2k+l\leq 2m\end{subarray}}\|\partial_{t}^{k}D^{l}_{x}u\|_{\infty}.

We will also use other types of iterated spaces. Let
X
be a Banach space and define

\displaystyle B([0,T],X)=\{u\colon[0,T]\to X\ |\ \sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t)\|_{X}<\infty\},

\displaystyle L^{\infty}([0,T],X)=\{u\colon[0,T]\to X\ |\ u\ {\rm is\ measurable\ and}\ \mathop{{\rm ess\,sup}}\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t)\|_{X}<\infty\},

\displaystyle L^{p}([0,T],X)=\{u\colon[0,T]\to X\ |\ u\ {\rm is\ measurable\ and}\ \int_{0}^{T}\|u(t)\|_{X}^{p}\,dt<\infty\}.

For the definition of measurability for Banach space-valued functions we refer, e.g., to
[
45
]
. The last two spaces above are sometimes called the Bochner spaces. We use the space
B([0,T],X)
to indicate that the function is defined and bounded for
every

t\in[0,T]
.

We recall some results concerning product measurability for
L^{p}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
p\in[1,\infty]
. First note that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
we have

(2.6)

\displaystyle C([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subseteq L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subseteq L^{p}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subseteq L^{1}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

By
[
68
, Theorems 4.4.1 and 4.4.3]
(see also
[
45
, Proposition 1.2.25]
), for every
u\in L^{1}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
there exists
\widetilde{u}\in L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
(in particular,
\widetilde{u}
is product measurable) such that for all
t\in[0,T]
we have
\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)=u(t)
a.e. Therefore, by identifying
u
with
\widetilde{u}
we can use Fubini’s theorem for functions in
L^{1}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, hence for all the functions in spaces appearing in (
2.6
).

We also note that the pairing of
\rho\in L^{1}([0,T],C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with
\phi\in L^{\infty}([0,T],C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
makes sense, in particular, the function
t\mapsto\langle\rho(t),\phi(t)\rangle
is measurable, see
[
29
, IV.1]
. Functions of this type will often appear in Section
5
.

2.2.
Space of probability measures

Definition 2.10
.

Let

{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\bigg\{\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):\|\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\ \sup\limits_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|}{|x-y|}\leq 1\bigg\}.

Following
[
10
, Vol. 2, Section 8.3]
, for
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define the Kantorovich–Rubinstein distance
d_{0}
as

d_{0}(m,m^{\prime})=\sup\limits_{\phi\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dx)\bigg|.

This metric is often called the bounded-Lipschitz metric, and should not be confused with Wasserstein metric
\mathcal{W}_{1}
.
1
1

1

The
\mathcal{W}_{1}
-metric is sometimes also called the Kantorovich–Rubinstein metric. The Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality gives a characterization of
\mathcal{W}_{1}
that is similar to
d_{0}
, but
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is a strictly stronger metric, requiring also convergence of first moments.

Below, whenever we consider
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as a metric space, we mean
(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{0})
. The above definition makes sense also for
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
m\mapsto d_{0}(m,0)
is a norm in
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 2.11
.

(a)

Convergence of probability measures in
d_{0}
is equivalent to their weak convergence (tested on
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions), see, e.g., Dudley
[
32
, Chapters 11.2 and 11.3]
. Consequently, by Prokhorov’s theorem, tight subsets of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are precompact.

(b)

By
[
49
, Theorems 4.19 and 17.23]
both
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
are complete.

(c)

In the Schauder-type fixed point arguments one usually needs to have a locally convex topological vector space containing
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Here, the space
(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{0})
shall serve this purpose. We note in passing that
(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{0})
is not complete, see
[
10
, Chapter 8.3]
, but the Schauder–Tychonoff theorem only requires a normed space, see, e.g.,
[
61
, Theorem 10.1]
.

(d)

Convergence of
signed
measures in
d_{0}
does not imply weak convergence or tightness, take for example
m_{n}=\delta_{n+\frac{1}{n}}-\delta_{n}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
f
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
2.7
and assume that there exist
\gamma\geq 0
and
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
such that
\rho\in C([0,T],C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\rho(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
t\in[0,T]
. Then
\rho\ast f\in C([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\{\rho(t)\ast f:t\in[0,T]\}
is a tight family.

Proof.

Since
\rho(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by Lemma
2.7
we get that
\rho(t)\ast f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Furthermore, for every
s,t\in[0,T]
and
\phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle|\langle\rho(t)\ast f-\rho(s)\ast f,\phi\rangle|\leq\|\rho(t)-\rho(s)\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi\ast\overline{f}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(f)\|\rho(t)-\rho(s)\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

thus from
\rho\in C([0,T],C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we get
\rho\ast f\in C([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Since
\{\rho(t)\ast f:t\in[0,T]\}
is a continuous image of a compact interval, it is compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, hence by Kolmogorov–Riesz theorem
[
43
, Theorem 5]
we get its tightness.
∎

The following result allows us to quantify the notion of tightness, with the help of generalized moments. The general idea is quite well-known, see e.g.,
[
10
, Example 8.6.5]
, but the form presented here obtained in
[
23
, Section 4.1]
, allows to use the function
\psi
below as a test function in the Fokker–Planck equation. This is crucial for proving preservation of tightness in Lemma
3.5
and Theorem
3.9
.

Lemma 2.13
.

A family
\mathcal{W}
of probability measures is tight if and only if there exists a constant
C>0
and a non-negative subadditive function
\psi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|D\psi\|_{\infty}
,
\|D^{2}\psi\|_{\infty}<\infty
,
\lim\limits_{|x|\to\infty}\psi(x)=\infty
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m(dx)\leq C,\quad m\in\mathcal{W}.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and denote
m_{\varepsilon}=m\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}
, where
\eta
is a standard mollifier. Then for every bounded uniformly continuous function
\phi
with the modulus of continuity
\omega_{\phi}
we have

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,(m_{\varepsilon}-m)(dx)\bigg|\leq\omega_{\phi}(\varepsilon).

As a consequence,

\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}d_{0}(m\ast\eta_{\varepsilon},m)=0.

Proof.

Let
\phi
be bounded and uniformly continuous. We have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,(m_{\varepsilon}-m)(dx)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,m(dy)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(y)\,m(dy)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,m(dy)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(y)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,m(dy)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,m(dy)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{B(x,\varepsilon)}\omega_{\phi}(|x-y|)\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\,dx\,m(dy)\leq\omega_{\phi}(\varepsilon).

Since
\omega_{\phi}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon
for every
\phi\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we get the convergence in
d_{0}
.
∎

The following standard result follows from Riesz’ representation theorem and mollification.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and assume that for every
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(m-m^{\prime})=0
. Then
m=m^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and assume that there exists
C>0
such that for every
\phi\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(m-m^{\prime})\bigg|\leq C.

Then
d_{0}(m,m^{\prime})\leq C
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\phi_{\varepsilon}=\phi\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(m-m^{\prime})\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\phi_{\varepsilon}(m-m^{\prime})\bigg|=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{\varepsilon}(m-m^{\prime})\bigg|\leq C.

∎

2.3.
Derivative in the space of probability measures

Definition 2.17
.

Let
U\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
. We say that
U
is
C^{1}
if there exists a continuous mapping
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m,\cdot)
is bounded for all
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the following relation holds for every
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\lim\limits_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{U(m+h(m^{\prime}-m))-U(m)}{h}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m,y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy).

Remark 2.18
.

(a)

From the above relation we see that
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
is not defined uniquely, as we may add to it any constant. Nonetheless, we
do not use
the normalization condition appearing commonly in the literature:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m,y)\,m(dy)=0,\quad m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In many of the cases below,
\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}
is either given explicitly, or predetermined. If it is not explicit, then by saying that
\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}
exists and has certain properties, we mean that there exists a version of
\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}
which has these properties. We drop the normalization as it interferes with the monotonicity condition for some natural examples of
F
and
G
, see Remark
2.23
below.

(b)

If
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
satisfy the definition of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
for all
m,m^{\prime}
and
y
, then by taking
m^{\prime}=\delta_{y_{1}}

and
m^{\prime}=\delta_{y_{2}}
and subtracting we find that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(m,y_{1})-\Phi_{1}(m,y_{2})=\Phi_{2}(m,y_{1})-\Phi_{2}(m,y_{2}),\quad m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Therefore, the existence and values of
D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m},D^{2}_{yy}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m},\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
do not depend on choice of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
. Note that this includes all the expressions appearing in the master equation.

Lemma 2.19
.

If
U\in C^{1}
then for every
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

(2.8)

U(m^{\prime})-U(m)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(\lambda m+(1-\lambda)m^{\prime},y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda.

Proof.

For
\lambda\in[0,1]
let
\Phi(\lambda)=U(m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m))
. If
\lambda\in(0,1)
, then by the definition of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m},

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{U(m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m)+h(m^{\prime}-m))-U(m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m))}{h}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy).

The statement follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus and the fact that
U(m^{\prime})-U(m)=\Phi(1)-\Phi(0).

∎

The following result has been stated in the
d_{1}
setting in
[
13
, p. 31]
. The proof for
d_{0}
is identical.

Lemma 2.20
.

Assume that
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
and
D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
are bounded. Then
U
is Lipschitz with respect to
m
and

\displaystyle|U(m_{1})-U(m_{2})|\leq\sup\limits_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg\|\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d_{0}(m_{1},m_{2}).

In the next lemma we discuss interchanging space and measure derivatives.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
U\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
D_{x}U
,
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
and
D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
exist and are continuous and bounded in all variables.
Then
\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}D_{x}U
exists and we can set

\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}D_{x}U(x,m,y)=D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m,y),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

Let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We follow the approach of Rudin for two real variables
[
66
, Theorem 9.41]
. By Lemma
2.19
and the mean value theorem

\displaystyle U(x^{\prime},m^{\prime})-U(x^{\prime},m)-U(x,m^{\prime})+U(x,m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x^{\prime},m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)-\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\bigg]\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(\xi,m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda,

where
\xi
belongs to the line segment connecting
x
and
x^{\prime}
. By subtracting

(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda

from both sides and by using the continuity and boundedness of
D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
and the dominated convergence theorem, we find that the expression

\displaystyle\bigg|U(x^{\prime},m^{\prime})-U(x^{\prime},m)-U(x,m^{\prime})+U(x,m)-(x^{\prime}-x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda\bigg|

is
o(|x-x^{\prime}|)
when
x^{\prime}\to x
.
Therefore

\displaystyle D_{x}U(x,m^{\prime})-D_{x}U(x,m)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m+\lambda(m^{\prime}-m),y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,d\lambda.

Thus, by taking
m^{\prime}=m+h(\widetilde{m}-m)
, using the boundedness and continuity in
m
of
D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
, and the dominated convergence theorem, we find that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{D_{x}U(x,m+h(\widetilde{m}-m))-D_{x}U(x,m)}{h}=\int_{0}^{1}D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)\,(\widetilde{m}-m)(dy).

This implies that
\frac{\delta{}}{\delta m}D_{x}U
exists and can be taken to be
D_{x}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
.
∎

2.4.
Operator
\mathcal{L}
and its heat kernel

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the Lévy operator given in (
1.2
). It is well-known (see, e.g., Khoshnevisan and Schilling
[
51
, Theorem 6.8]
) that
\mathcal{L}
can be represented as a Fourier multiplier
\Psi
, i.e., for all
u\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}u(x)=-\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\Psi\mathcal{F}u)(x),

where
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform. The multiplier
\Psi
is also called the characteristic exponent or the symbol. By the Lévy–Khintchine representation theorem we have

\displaystyle\Psi(\xi)=-iB\xi+\xi A\xi+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-e^{i\xi z}+i\xi z\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(z))\,\nu(dz),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The adjoint operator has symbol
\overline{\Psi}(\xi)=iB\xi+\xi A\xi+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-e^{i\xi z}+i\xi z\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(z))\,\nu(-dz),

therefore if we let
\widetilde{\nu}(E)=\nu(-E)
for Borel sets
E
, we get that for
u\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{L}^{\ast}u(x)=-B\cdot Du(x)+\Div(ADu(x))+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u(x+z)-u(x)-Du(x)\cdot z\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(z))\,\widetilde{\nu}(dz),

and for
u,v\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\mathcal{L}v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}^{*}uv.

By considering a smooth cut-off function we can extend the above formula to
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(note that
\mathcal{L}v\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
p\geq 1
). Furthermore, by using the cut-off once more, we may show that it also holds if
u\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in C^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
v,\mathcal{L}v\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

For functions in
C^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, operator
\mathcal{L}
agrees with the generator of the semigroup corresponding to the transition probabilities of Lévy process
X_{t}
with Lévy triplet
(B,A,\nu)
, see
[
67
, Theorem 31.5]
. The transition probability is given by the formula

\displaystyle K(0)=\delta_{0},\qquad K(t,x)\,dx=(\mathcal{F}^{-1}e^{-t\Psi})(x)\,dx,\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We will sometimes use the notation
K_{t}(x)=K(t,x)
. Recall that
K(t,\cdot)\,dx
is a probability measure for every
t\geq 0
. We
assume
that
K(t,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
t>0
2
2

2

This is implied by assumption
(K)
below. Note that for general Lévy operators
K(t,\cdot)
may be a singular measure, e.g., when the Lévy triplet equals
(0,0,\delta_{x_{0}})
or
(B,0,0)
.
, which implies that
K(t,\cdot)
is in the domain of the generator, so we can infer that
K
solves the heat equation for
\mathcal{L}
pointwise:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}K(t,x)=\mathcal{L}K(t,x),\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In addition we have
\|K_{t}\ast f-f\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
, for every
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
— this is the strong continuity of the semigroup corresponding to
X_{t}
. From now on
K
will be called the heat kernel of
\mathcal{L}
. Note that the heat kernel
K_{t}^{\ast}
of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
satisfies
K_{t}^{\ast}(x)=K_{t}(-x)
. Recall that if
\mathcal{L}=\Delta
, then

\displaystyle K_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}},\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

For most operators
\mathcal{L}
the heat kernel does not have an explicit formula.

2.5.
Assumptions

2.5.1.
The Hamiltonian

The set of assumptions on the Hamiltonian
H
is fairly standard, see
[
2
,
34
,
56
]
.

(H1)

H\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and for every
l\in\mathbb{N}^{2d+1}
with
|l|\leq 4
,
\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{l}H(x,\cdot,\cdot)|
is locally bounded.

(H2)

For every
R>0
there exists
C_{R}>0
such that for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
|u|\leq R
, and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|H(x,u,p)-H(y,u,p)|\leq C_{R}(1+|p|)|x-y|.

(H3)

There exists
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
u\leq v
, and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

H(x,v,p)-H(x,u,p)\geq\gamma(v-u).

2.5.2.
The operator

The assumption on the diffusion operator
\mathcal{L}
is given in terms of its heat kernel.

(K)

There is
\mathcal{K}>0
and
\underline{\alpha}\in(1,2]
, such that the heat kernels
K
and
K^{*}
of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}
respectively are smooth densities of probability measures, and for
\tilde{K}=K,K^{*}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
we have

\|D^{\beta}\tilde{K}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\mathcal{K}t^{-\frac{|\beta|}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

Remark 2.22
.

(a)

Assumption
(K)
implies that
K(t,\cdot)\in W^{n,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. From the Sobolev embeddings it follows that
D^{\beta}K(t,\cdot)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t>0
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, and
1<p\leq\infty
, and as a consequence
K(t,\cdot)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t>0
.

(b)

We note that
\|\mathcal{L}u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\mathcal{L})(\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D^{2}u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})
and
\|\mathcal{L}u\|_{\infty}\leq C(\mathcal{L})\|u\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
hold for any Lévy operator
\mathcal{L}
. The condition
[
34
, L2(i)]
yields a more precise bound on
\mathcal{L}u
(see Lemma 2.1 therein), but we do not adopt this assumption, as the only gain from it are slightly better bounds for time regularity, which are not needed in our work.

(c)

We will often use integration by parts in integrals involving
K
. Since
K(t)
and
D_{y}K(t)
are integrable and smooth with all derivatives bounded, we get that
\int_{\partial B_{r}}K(t)
vanishes at infinity as a function of
r
. Therefore, for any
\phi\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t,x-y)\phi(y)\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t,x-y)D_{y}\phi(y)\,dy.

(d)

We can interpolate the bounds on derivatives in
(K)
to get similar bounds for Hölder quotients. In order to see that, take
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with both
f
and
Df
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and note that for any
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x+h)-f(x)|^{p}}{|h|^{p\sigma}}\,dx\leq|h|^{p(1-\sigma)}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Df(x+\lambda h)|^{p}\,dx\,d\lambda=|h|^{p(1-\sigma)}\|Df\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x+h)-f(x)|^{p}}{|h|^{p\sigma}}\,dx\leq 2^{p}|h|^{-p\sigma}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p},

therefore

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{|h|>0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x+h)-f(x)|^{p}}{|h|^{p\sigma}}\,dx\leq\sup\limits_{|h|>0}\big(|h|^{p(1-\sigma)}\|Df\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\wedge 2^{p}|h|^{-p\sigma}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\big)=2^{p(1-\sigma)}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p(1-\sigma)}\|Df\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p\sigma}.

2.5.3.
Functions
F
and
G

In the following assumptions we allow
k\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
and
\sigma\in[0,1)
, but for the sake of the main results we only need
k=1
and a fixed
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
.

(F1)

F\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\sup\limits_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|F(\cdot,m)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty,

\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,m\neq m^{\prime}}\frac{|F(x,m)-F(x,m^{\prime})|}{d_{0}(m,m^{\prime})}<\infty.

(F2(k,
\sigma
))

There exists
C>0
such that for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq C,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)-\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m^{\prime},\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{0}(m,m^{\prime}).

(G1(
\sigma
))

G\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\sup\limits_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|G(\cdot,m)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty,

\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,m\neq m^{\prime}}\frac{|G(x,m)-G(x,m^{\prime})|}{d_{0}(m,m^{\prime})}<\infty.

(G2(k,
\sigma
))

There exists
C>0
such that for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq C,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)-\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m^{\prime},\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{0}(m,m^{\prime}).

2.5.4.
Monotonicity conditions

(M1)

The Lasry–Lions monotonicity condition
[
53
]
holds for
F
and
G
, that is, for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F(x,m^{\prime})-F(x,m))(m^{\prime}-m)(dx)\geq 0,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(G(x,m^{\prime})-G(x,m))(m^{\prime}-m)(dx)\geq 0.

(M2)

(F2(k,

σ

))
and
(G2(k,

σ

))
hold and for every
\rho\in C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{F(\cdot,m,\cdot)}}{\delta m},\rho\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho\bigg\rangle_{x}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{G(\cdot,m,\cdot)}}{\delta m},\rho\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho\bigg\rangle_{x}\geq 0,

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{x},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{y}
are the pairings between
C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
x
and
y
respectively.

Remark 2.23
.

The assumption
(M2)
is stronger than
(M1)
, in fact, strictly stronger, if the normalization condition (
2.7
) is assumed. In order to show that
(M2)
implies
(M1)
, it suffices to take
\rho=m^{\prime}-m
and use the fundamental theorem of calculus of Lemma
2.19
. Though it seems plausible, it is unclear to us if there is a universal way to pick/normalize
\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}
so that
(M1)
implies
(M2)
. The results of Sections
5
and
6
could be modified to only require
\rho
with mean zero in the sense that
\langle\rho,1\rangle=0
, but we were unable to prove that
(M1)
implies
(M2)
for such
\rho
– even if we assume they are nice (e.g. measures with smooth densities and integral 0).

Example 2.24
.

Let
F(x,m)=\phi\ast m(x)
for some nontrivial odd function
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F(x,m_{1})-F(x,m_{2}))\,(m_{1}-m_{2})(dx)=0,\quad m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

so
(M1)
is satisfied. Under the normalization condition (
2.7
) we have (compare that with (
2.11
))
3
3

3

Accordingly, the derivative in
[
35
, Example 1.1]
should read
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(m)(y)=h(y)-\int h(y)\,m(dy)
.

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)=\phi(x-y)-\phi\ast m(x).

Let
m=\delta_{0}
and
\rho=\delta_{x_{0}}
for some
x_{0}\neq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{F(\cdot,m,\cdot)}}{\delta m},\rho\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho\bigg\rangle_{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)\,\delta_{x_{0}}(dy)\,\delta_{x_{0}}(dx)=\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x_{0},m,x_{0})=\phi(0)-\phi\ast m(x_{0})=\phi(0)-\phi(x_{0}).

Since
\phi
is odd we have
\phi(0)=0
, and since it is non-trivial, the above expression does not have a constant sign for all
x_{0}
. Hence
(M2)
is not satisfied.

(M3)

H(x,u,p)=H(x,p)
and there exists
c_{1}\geq 1
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\frac{1}{c_{1}}I_{d}\leq D^{2}_{pp}H(x,\cdot)\leq c_{1}I_{d}.

(M4)

H(x,u,p)=H_{1}(x,p)+H_{2}(x,u)
and there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\frac{1}{c_{1}}I_{d}\leq D^{2}_{pp}H_{1}(x,\cdot)\leq c_{1}I_{d},

and

0\leq D_{u}H_{2}(x,\cdot)\leq c_{2}.

Remark 2.25
.

Assumption
(M3)
is strictly stronger than
(M4)
. The latter yields a weaker variant of the Lasry–Lions monotonicity lemma (Lemma
4.2
) which suffices for obtaining uniqueness for the MFG system, but seems ineffective in terms of stability results. That is why at some point in the paper we need to adopt
(M3)
, which implies the more standard version of the monotonicity lemma (see Remark
5.4
).

2.6.
Examples

2.6.1.
The operator
\mathcal{L}

Recall that
\mathcal{L}
is determined by its Lévy triplet
(A,B,\nu)
. Here are some concrete assumptions on operators or the Lévy triplets, under which
(K)
holds, taken mostly from
[
34
, Section 4]
.

•

If
\mathcal{L}
satisfies
(K)
and
L
is any Lévy operator, then
\mathcal{L}+L
satisfies
(K)
.
4
4

4

Hence it suffices to specify the assumptions only on
A
or
\nu
, or even a part of
\nu
. The remaining terms in the Lévy triplet can be arbitrary, in particular they can vanish or be degenerate.

•

The diffusion matrix
A
satisfies
\langle Ax,x\rangle\geq|x|^{2}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, e.g.,
\mathcal{L}=\Delta
. Then
(K)
is satisfied with
\underline{\alpha}=2
.
5
5

5

This was not discussed in
[
34
]
, but can easily be checked by hand for the Gaussian heat kernel
\mathcal{G}_{t}
. For nondegenerate
A
, the heat kernel corresponding to
\Div(ADu)
is equal to
K_{t}(x)=|A|^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{t}(Ax)
, so the estimate follows from the Gaussian case.

•

The Lévy measure
\nu
has an absolutely continuous part with density
\nu_{ac}
such that
\nu_{ac}(z)\approx|z|^{-d-\underline{\alpha}}
for all
|z|\leq 1
and
\underline{\alpha}\in(1,2)
, see
[
34
, Theorem 4.3]
6
6

6

The proof of this result is based on the heat kernel estimates in
[
42
, Theorem 5.2]
.
.

•

\mathcal{L}=-(-\partial^{2}_{x_{1}})^{\alpha_{1}/2}-(-\partial^{2}_{x_{2}})^{\alpha_{2}/2}-\ldots-(-\partial^{2}_{x_{d}})^{\alpha_{d}/2}
, where
\alpha_{i}\in(1,2)
. Then
\underline{\alpha}=\min_{i}\alpha_{i}
.

•

The Riesz–Feller operator on
\mathbb{R}
:
\nu(z)=|z|^{-1-\underline{\alpha}}\textbf{1}_{(0,\infty)}(z)
, see
[
3
, Lemma 2.1 (G7) and Proposition 2.3]
.

•

The CGMY model operator, see
[
34
, Example 4.4]
.

2.6.2.
The coupling terms
F
and
G

We will only discuss
F
, as the assumptions for
G
are identical up to the order of differentiation. We now give two (slightly) different examples. Define

(2.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1}(x,m)=\phi_{1}\ast m(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\[5.69046pt]
\qquad\text{for $\phi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ positive semi-definite (see Definition~\ref{def:posdef} below).}\end{cases}

Examples similar to
F_{1}
are studied in
[
16
, Chapter 3, Example 5]
in connection with
(M1)
. Following
[
13
]
, we let

(2.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{2}(x,m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\,\phi_{2}(x-z)\,dz,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\[5.69046pt]
\qquad\text{for $\phi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ non-negative and even, and }\\
\qquad\quad\text{$\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ smooth, $\Phi(z,s)$ and $\partial_{s}\Phi(z,s)$ are bounded in $z$, locally uniformly in $s$, and $\partial_{s}\Phi(z,s)\geq 0$.}\end{cases}

We now check that our assumptions are satisfied. Starting with
F_{1}
in (
2.9
) we note that

(2.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{F_{1}}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)=\phi_{1}(x-y),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

satisfies Definition
2.17
. It is then obvious that
(F1)
and
(F2(k,

σ

))
are satisfied. The monotonicity properties of
F_{1}
and
\frac{\delta{F_{1}}}{\delta m}
are closely related to the positive definiteness of
\phi_{1}
.

Definition 2.26
.

We say that a function
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is positive semi-definite, if it is even and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}\phi(x_{i}-x_{j})\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\geq 0,\quad x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R},

Lemma 2.27
.

The function
F_{1}
defined by (
2.9
) satisfies
(M1)
and
(M2)
.

Proof.

Since
\phi_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by the well known characterization of positive-definiteness
[
9
, p. 93]
for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x-y)f(x)f(y)\,dy\,dx\geq 0.

Let
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
m_{\varepsilon}=(m_{1}-m_{2})\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon>0
. Then
m_{\varepsilon}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and by smoothness of
\phi_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F(x,m_{1})-F(x,m_{2}))\,(m_{1}(dx)-m_{2}(dx))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x-y)\,(m_{1}(dy)-m_{2}(dy))(m_{1}(dx)-m_{2}(dx))

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x-y)\,m_{\varepsilon}(dx)m_{\varepsilon}(dy)\geq 0.

This proves that
F
satisfies
(M1)
. In order to get
(M2)
we first note that by Lemmas
2.3
and
2.8
, and the fact that
\phi
is smooth, we get that for any
\gamma\geq 0
and
\rho\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\langle\langle\phi_{1}(x-y),\rho\rangle_{y},\rho\rangle_{x}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\langle\langle\phi_{1}(x-y),\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{y},\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{x}.

Also by Lemma
2.3
, we have
\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\in C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so there exists
\mu_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\langle f,\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\int f\mu_{\varepsilon}
for every
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, since
\phi_{1}(x-\cdot)\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1}(x-y),\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x-y)\,\mu_{\varepsilon}(dy)=:\Phi(x)\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This implies that

\displaystyle\langle\langle\phi_{1}(x-y),\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{y},\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{x}=\langle\Phi,\rho\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Phi(x)\,\mu_{\varepsilon}(dx)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{1}(x-y)\,\mu_{\varepsilon}(dy)\mu_{\varepsilon}(dx)\geq 0,

which proves
(M2)
.
∎

We now discuss
F_{2}
defined in (
2.10
). It was shown in
[
13
]
that it satisfies
(M1)
.

Lemma 2.28
.

The function
F_{2}
defined in (
2.10
) has a version of
\frac{\delta{F_{2}}}{\delta m}
which satisfies
(M2)
.

Proof.

We first note that for
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{F_{2}(x,m+h(m^{\prime}-m))-F_{2}(x,m)}{h}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\lim\limits_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\phi_{2}\ast(m+h(m^{\prime}-m))(z)-\phi_{2}\ast m(z)}{h}\phi_{2}(x-z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\phi_{2}(x-z)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{2}(z-y)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy)\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\phi_{2}(x-z)\phi_{2}(z-y)\,dz\,(m^{\prime}-m)(dy),

so we can take

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{F_{2}}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\phi_{2}(x-z)\phi_{2}(z-y)\,dz.

By the proof of Lemma
2.27
it suffices to verify
(M2)
only for
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By Fubini’s theorem we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{F_{2}}}{\delta m}(x,m,y)\,\mu(dy)\,\mu(dx)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))\phi_{2}(x-z)\phi_{2}(z-y)\,dz\,\mu(dy)\,\mu(dx)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\Phi(z,(\phi_{2}\ast m)(z))(\phi_{2}\ast\mu(z))^{2}\,dz\geq 0.

∎

2.6.3.
The Hamiltonian
H

One prominent example satisfying all our assumptions is the quadratic Hamiltonian
H(x,u,p)=H(p)=|p|^{2}
. We can also consider

\displaystyle H(x,p)=f_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)g_{1}(p)+g_{2}(p),

where
f_{1}\in C^{4}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
g_{2}
is smooth and satisfies
(M3)
,
f_{2}\in C^{4}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is sufficiently small,
g_{1}
is smooth with at most linear growth. Such
H
satisfies assumptions
(H1)
–
(H3)
and
(M3)
as well.

If we let

\displaystyle H(x,u,p)=H_{1}(x,p)+H_{2}(x,u),

with
H_{1}
satisfying the assumptions above, we can consider e.g.
H_{2}(x,u)=f(x)g(u)
, where
f\in C^{4}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is nonnegative (e.g.
f\equiv 1
) and
g\in C^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
g^{\prime}\geq\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
(H1)
–
(H3)
are satisfied. Some examples are
g(u)=\beta u
for

\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, or when
u\geq 0
,
g(u)=(1+u^{2})^{q/2}

for

q\in\mathbb{R}

. In order to have
(M4)
needed for uniqueness of solutions to the MFG system, it suffices to have
0\leq g^{\prime}\leq c_{2}
, which is the case e.g. for
g(u)=\beta u
with
\beta>0
and for non-negative
u
,
g(u)=(1+u^{2})^{q/2}
with
q\in(0,1)
.

2.7.
Modifications needed in existing proofs/assumptions

2.7.1.
Approximations in Hölder spaces

Let
\gamma\geq 0
and
K\in\{\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{T}^{d},\Omega\}
, where
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a smooth domain. We first note that

C^{\infty}_{b}(K)\cap C^{\pm\gamma}_{b}(K)
is in general not dense in
C^{\pm\gamma}_{b}(K)
.
There are some exceptions for which the density holds, e.g.,
C_{b}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, but in most cases it does not hold – here are some counterexamples.

•

For
C^{-0}_{b}(K)
we consider
\delta_{0}
: any
\rho\in C_{b}(K)
can be identified with an element in
C^{-0}_{b}(K)
via
\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\int\rho(x)\phi(x)\,dx
, and then by taking test functions
\phi_{\varepsilon}\in C_{b}(K)
such that
0\leq\phi_{\varepsilon}\leq 1
,
\phi_{\varepsilon}(0)=1
, and
\int\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx<\varepsilon
, we get
|\langle\rho,\phi_{\varepsilon}\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|\rho\|_{\infty}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\rho-\delta_{0}\|_{C^{-0}_{b}(K)}\geq\frac{|\langle\delta_{0}-\rho,\phi_{\varepsilon}\rangle|}{\|\phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}}=\langle\delta_{0}-\rho,\phi_{\varepsilon}\rangle\geq 1-\delta,

where
\delta
can be arbitrarily small.

•

A counterexample for
C^{\gamma}_{b}(K)
with
\gamma\in(0,1)
is the function
\mathbb{R}\ni x\mapsto(x\vee 0)^{\gamma}
, see also
[
1
]
.

•

For
C^{-\gamma}_{b}((-1,1))
with
\gamma\in(0,1)
consider the functional
\rho
defined as

\displaystyle\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\lim\limits_{x\to 0^{+}}\frac{\phi(x)}{x^{\gamma}}

for
\phi
in the subspace of functions in
C^{\gamma}_{b}((-1,1))
for which the above limit exists and is finite. Then extend
\rho
to the whole
C^{\gamma}_{b}((-1,1))
by the Hahn–Banach theorem. If
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)=x^{\gamma}\textbf{1}_{[0,\varepsilon)}(x)+\varepsilon^{\gamma}\textbf{1}_{(\varepsilon,1)}(x)
for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
x\in(-1,1)
, then
\|\phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}((-1,1))}\in[1,2]
and
\langle\rho,\phi_{\varepsilon}\rangle=1
. Therefore, for any
\widetilde{\rho}\in C_{b}((-1,1))
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\rho}-\rho\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}((-1,1))}\geq\frac{|\langle\widetilde{\rho}-\rho,\phi_{\varepsilon}\rangle|}{\|\phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\bigg|1-\int\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\widetilde{\rho}(x)\,dx\bigg|,

which is greater than
\frac{1}{4}
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

•

The above counterexamples can also be used to show lack of density for
C^{-n}
,
C^{\gamma}
, and
C^{-\gamma}
spaces for
n=1,2,\ldots
and non-integer
\gamma>0
.

The lack of density has the following consequences:

(a)

(Time continuity in Schauder estimates) The solutions of
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)u=0
with the initial condition in
u_{0}\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(K)
(
\sigma\in(0,1)
) are in general not of class
C([0,T],C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(K))
7
7

7

The parabolic regularity
C^{1+\sigma/2,2+\sigma}
given by Schauder estimates does not imply that the
C^{2+\sigma}_{b}
norms are continuous up to 0.
8
8

8

But they are in
C_{b}((0,T],C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(K))
and
C([0,T],C^{2+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(K))
for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
.
. If that was the case, then the instantaneous smoothing effect
9
9

9

For example, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the solution is given by the convolution with the smooth heat kernel.
would imply that any
u_{0}\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(K)
can be approximated by smooth functions
u(t,\cdot)
in
C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(K)
, which contradicts the lack of density.
Due to this, some changes are needed in formulations of e.g.
[
12
,
13
,
64
,
71
]
. This also means that the Hölder regularity in time in
[
13
, Lemma 3.2.2]
10
10

10

The last inequality on page 59 is not true.
does not hold,
similar for
[
41
, Corollary 5.21]
.

(b)

(Approximation of data in negative Hölder spaces) In the linear system result (see Theorem
5.5
below) the coefficients in negative order Hölder spaces are approximated by smooth functions. This works fine as long as the coefficient has a slightly better regularity than the norm it is approximated in, see Lemma
2.3
, but the approximation of initial conditions of class
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(K)
by smooth functions in the
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(K)
norm in
[
13
, Lemma 3.3.1]
(see also
[
12
,
28
,
41
,
64
,
71
]
) is not possible. We avoid this problem by considering initial conditions with
C^{-k-1}_{b}
regularity, which is sufficient for all the cases where Theorem
5.5
is used. Another solution would be to drop norm convergence and use weak-
\ast
and the Banach–Alaoglu theorem. However, making this argument work seems cumbersome because of the lack of time equicontinuity of the approximate solutions in
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(K)
.

(c)

(Pathological functionals in negative Hölder spaces) Working with the negative order Hölder spaces on non-compact sets poses yet another problem with density: the convolution of a functional with a
C_{c}^{\infty}
function need not even be a measure. This is the case for functionals supported at infinity like the Banach limit, see Remark
2.4
. Therefore, the approximation arguments in
[
41
, Theorem 5.28]
and
[
12
, Proposition 5.5]
need to be changed. We solve the issue of pathological functionals by requiring the data to have representations in terms of integrals, see Definition
2.5
. This requires some work, because whenever we apply Theorem
5.5
, we need to ensure that the term
c
has this property.

(d)

(Compactness arguments for the linearized system) The discussion of data approximation and obtaining a compact Leray–Schauder map in
[
12
, Proposition 5.5]
needs modification, since the whole space requires different compactness arguments than the torus. For
K
of infinite Lebesgue measure, all of
C^{\gamma}_{b}(K)
cannot be embedded into

C^{-\gamma}_{b}(K)
because
\langle f,\phi\rangle=\int_{K}f\phi
does not converge for all
f,\phi\in C^{\gamma}_{b}(K)
, and compactness argumets using the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem are no longer appropriate. Instead, we use the
L^{1}
setting and the Kolmogorov–Riesz theorem, see the discussion following (
2.1
).
The
C^{\gamma}_{b}
norm works on the torus only because on sets of finite measure the supremum norm dominates the
L^{1}
norm.
The above discussion is also relevant for
[
41
, Theorem 5.28]
. We also note that the proof of
[
41
, Lemma B.1]
states that the parabolic space
C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}([0,T]\times(0,\infty))
is compactly embedded in
C^{2,1}([0,T]\times(0,\infty))
, which is not true.

2.7.2.
Insufficient regularity assumptions

(a)

The proof of the existence of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
in
[
13
, Proposition 3.4.3]
requires
n\geq 1
instead of
n\geq 0
, because the estimate
\|m-\widehat{m}\|_{-n-\alpha}\leq d_{1}(m,\widehat{m})
at the end of the proof does not hold with
n=0
.

(b)

The main “bottleneck” in the study of the well-posedness of the master equation is the continuity in
m
(or in
t
) of
D^{2}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
. In
[
13
]
the proof is skipped, whereas in
[
64
]
this property is used without mention in the density argument in the proof of Theorem 2.5. In fact a stronger Lipschitz continuity result is proved in
[
13
]
in connection with the convergence problem, after the proof of well-posedness for the master equation (see Proposition 3.6.1). It requires
n\geq 2
in their setting, which enforces quite strong assumptions. We adapt and modify the idea of Ricciardi
[
64
]
to allow blow-up of norms at time
t_{0}
, and prove
Lipschitz continuity in
m
with less assumptions – corresponding roughly to
n\geq 1
in
[
13
]
.

One could expect that the excessive assumptions used in
[
13
, Proposition 3.6.1]
appear because Lipschitz continuity is stronger than mere continuity, but the main obstacle here seems to be the structure of the generic linear system result. The expression
D^{2}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(\cdot,\cdot,m_{0}^{1},\cdot)-D^{2}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(\cdot,\cdot,m_{0}^{2},\cdot)
satisfies the linear system (
5.7
) with
c\in B([t_{0},T],C^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(see
c_{1}
in (
5.33
), where
\rho^{2}
satisfies the initial condition
\rho^{2}(t_{0})=\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}
). In the framework of
[
13
, Lemma 3.3.1]
they therefore need to take
n=2
, whether to prove just continuity or Lipschitz continuity. Hence the proof of
[
13
, Theorem 2.4.2]
really requires
n\geq 2
.

For
[
64
]
(see also
[
28
,
71
]
) this has further consequences, as the linear system result in
[
64
, Proposition 5.8]
is insufficient to prove the main results as the estimates depend on
\|c\|_{L^{1}([t_{0},T]\times\Omega)}
. However for the
c
mentioned above, we can only expect to have
\|c(t)\|_{L^{1}}\leq C(t-t_{0})^{-1}
, which is not integrable in time. We fix the approach of Ricciardi
[
64
]
by using more integrability in time at the expense of less regularity in space:
in Theorem
5.5
we allow
c\in L^{1}([t_{0},T],C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Then by using Lemma
5.7
we ensure that every
c
we encounter (including above) is in
L^{1}([t_{0},T],C^{-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, so that we only need
k=n=1
for well-posedness.
11
11

11

In fact Lemma
5.7
gives even better regularity than
C^{-1}
, but for various technical reasons we were unable to work with
n=0
.

2.7.3.
Monotonicity and normalization

The cross-multiplication estimate in the proof of the linear system result
[
13
, Lemma 3.3.1]
(see also
[
28
,
64
,
71
]
) tacitly uses the property (as above,
K\in\{\Omega,\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}\})

(2.12)

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{F(\cdot,m,\cdot)}}{\delta m},\rho\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho\bigg\rangle_{x}\geq 0,\quad\rho\in C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(K),

with the claim that it follows from the standard monotonicity condition

(2.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(F(x,m_{1})-F(x,m_{2}))\,(m_{1}-m_{2})(dx)\geq 0,\quad m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(K).

As we argue in Example
2.24
, this is not the case, therefore (
2.12
) became a separate assumption in our paper. It turns out quite tedious to verify; in Section
2.6
we show that both conditions are satisfied for standard examples in the literature, but to this end we need to drop the usual normalization condition (
2.7
), see Remark
2.18
.

2.7.4.
Stability in
t_{0}
for the MFG system

In order to prove continuity in
t_{0}
for
U
and its derivatives in Proposition
5.10
we consider a linearized system with the terms corresponding to solutions of the MFG system with the same initial measure on different time intervals
[t_{1},T]
and
[t_{2},T]
. This requires an appropriate stability result with respect to
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, which we give in Lemma
D.1
. This is not a great issue and it does not impose any additional assumptions, but it seems to be overlooked in the literature.

3.
Well-posedness and regularity for single equations
