In this section we establish well-posedness and regularity for several linear equations. We also recall results of
[
34
]
on Fokker–Planck and Hamilton–Jacobi equations and prove new and refined variants of these results: better (almost optimal) regularising effect, (much) weaker assumptions on the data, and mixed local-nonlocal operators. In
[
34
]
purely nonlocal diffusions are treated, but the results which are required for our present purposes are valid also for local and mixed local-nonlocal diffusions – see Theorem
3.6
, Remark
3.7
, and Theorem
3.10
with proof.

Remark 3.1
.

The results of this section are given for equations on the time interval
(0,T)
, but since
T
is arbitrary, they are true also on the intervals of the form
(t_{0},T)
with any
t_{0}\in(0,T)
. The constants in the estimates may depend on
T
, but when
T
is fixed, the constants for equations on
(t_{0},T)
do not depend on
t_{0}
.

Almost all the results of this section are obtained with the help of Duhamel’s formula and Banach’s fixed point theorem. Using these tools we obtain mild solutions and then we argue that they in fact solve the equation in the desired sense. We say that
u
is a mild solution to the problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u+\mathcal{H}(t,x,u,Du)=0,\quad&(t,x)\in(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(0)=u_{0},\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

if it satisfies

\displaystyle u(t,x)=K_{t}\ast u_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}K(t-s,\cdot)\ast\mathcal{H}(s,\cdot,u(s,\cdot),Du(s,\cdot))\,dy\,ds.

We will differentiate the above formula to get bounds for derivatives of
u
. Formally,

\displaystyle D_{x}u(t,x)=K_{t}\ast D_{x}u_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}(D_{x}K)(t-s,\cdot)\ast\mathcal{H}(s,\cdot,u(s,\cdot),Du(s,\cdot))\,dy\,ds.

By
(K)
we have
\|D_{x}K(t-s)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\mathcal{K}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}
. This motivates the need for the following Grönwall-type inequality.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
69
, Corollary 2]
)
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
,
\gamma>0
and assume that
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle f(t)\leq a+b\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{\gamma-1}f(s)\,ds,\quad t\in[0,T].

Then
f(t)\leq c(T,b)\cdot a
for all
t\in[0,T]
, with
c
locally bounded.

3.1.
Schauder theory for linear equations

Various forms of regularity estimates for parabolic equations with nonlocal Lévy-type operators have been known for quite a long time, but we include the proof for completeness and in order to demonstrate that
(K)
is tailor-made for this result.

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that
(K)
holds, let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
,
b\in(C([0,T],C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
,
z_{0}\in C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some natural number
k\geq 2
, and
f\in C([0,T],C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Then,

(a)

the problem

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}z-\mathcal{L}z-b(t,x)Dz=f(t,x)\quad&{\rm in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
z(0)=z_{0}&{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases}

has a unique classical solution
z\in B([0,T],C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([0,T],C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
), which satisfies

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}z\|_{\infty}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}),

where
C
is independent of
\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

(b)

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a modulus of continuity
\omega
independent of
f
and
z_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\|z(s,\cdot)-z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq(\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\omega(t-s),\quad s,t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}z(s,\cdot)-\partial_{t}z(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\omega(t-s),\quad s,t\in[0,T].

(c)

There exists
C>0
which depends only on
T
,
\mathcal{K}
from
(K)
and
\|b\|_{\infty}
, such that

(3.3)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\|z_{0}\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|f\|_{\infty}).

Remark 3.4
.

It should be possible to prove this result with
\sigma=\underline{\alpha}-1
, but it would require more work and more precise assumptions on the heat kernel. One needs to have stronger scaling properties in order to isolate and treat singularities separately from the integrable part of the integrands, see Priola
[
63
, Theorem 3.4]
for stable diffusions, or the book of Gilbarg and Trudinger
[
38
, Lemma 4.4]
for local elliptic equations.

Proof.

(a) The proof is based on Duhamel’s formula and uses Banach fixed point arguments similar to those in
[
34
, Sections 5 and 6]
. We let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\sigma)
and we define

\displaystyle X=B([0,T],C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([0,T],C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

and for
\widetilde{z}\in X
we say that
S(\widetilde{z})=z
, if for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle z(t,x)=K_{t}\ast z_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)b(s,y)D\widetilde{z}(s,y)\,dy\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)f(s,y)\,dy\,ds.

We will now estimate spatial
C^{k+\sigma}
norms of
z
. First, using integration by parts, the triangle inequality, and
(K)
we get

\displaystyle|z(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{\infty}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}\big(K(t-s,x-y)b(s,y)\big)||\widetilde{z}(s,y)|\,dy\,ds+T\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{\infty}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\widetilde{z}(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}\big(K(t-s,x-y)b(s,y)\big)|\,dy\,ds+T\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{\infty}+T\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty},

where
C=C(K,T)
and
C(T)\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{\infty}+T\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}.

By differentiating (
3.4
) with respect to
x
we get

(3.5)

\displaystyle Dz(t,x)=K_{t}\ast Dz_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)b(s,y)D\widetilde{z}(s,y)\,dy\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)f(s,y)\,dy\,ds.

The interchange of the derivative and the double integrals above is justified as follows: we use the fundamental theorem of calculus on the difference quotients, the Fubini–Tonelli theorem, and we change the variables, getting expressions of the type:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{1}D_{x}K(t-s,x+\lambda h-y)f(s,y)\,d\lambda\,dy\,ds=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)f(s,y+\lambda h)\,dy\,ds\,d\lambda,

where
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,
|D_{x}K|\|f\|_{\infty}
is a majorizing function (it is integrable by
(K)
) and so (
3.5
) follows from the dominated convergence theorem.

By using
(K)
in (
3.5
) we find that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|Dz(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C(T)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}+C(T)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
C(T)\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
. The procedure for the second order derivatives of
z
is as follows: we use integration by parts in both integrals in (
3.5
) and then we differentiate once more. If we repeat that for the next derivatives, we get that for
1\leq l\leq k
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle D^{l}z(t,x)=K_{t}\ast D^{l}z_{0}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)D^{l-1}\big(b(s,y)D\widetilde{z}(s,y)\big)\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)D^{l-1}f(s,y)\,dy\,ds.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|D^{l}z(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{l}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C(T)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{l-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C(T)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{l-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{l}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
C(T)\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
.
We now proceed to the Hölder norms. For any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{|D^{k}z(t,x+h)-D^{k}z(t,x)|}{|h|^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|D_{x}K(t-s,x+h-y)-D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)|}{|h|^{\sigma}}|D^{k-1}\big(b(s,y)D\widetilde{z}(s,y)\big)|\,dy\,ds

(3.7)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|D_{x}K(t-s,x+h-y)-D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)|}{|h|^{\sigma}}|D^{k-1}f(s,y)|\,dy\,ds.

Therefore, by Remark
2.22
(c) and
(K)
we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|D^{k}z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle+C\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\int_{0}^{T}\|D^{2}_{x}K(s)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\sigma}\|D_{x}K(s)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1-\sigma}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\int_{0}^{T}s^{-\frac{1+\sigma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C(T)\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

where
C(T)\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
, because
1+\sigma<\underline{\alpha}
. Note that
C(T)
blows up as
\sigma\to(\underline{\alpha}-1)^{-}
, but stays bounded as long as
\sigma
is separated from
\underline{\alpha}-1
.

By summing up the above estimates we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C(T)\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{z}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

where
C(T)\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
. The proof that
\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is continuous in
t
is postponed to the appendix, see Lemma
B.2
. We get that
z=S(\widetilde{z})\in X
. Furthermore, since
S
is an affine map, we get that it is a contraction if
T
is small enough (note that
z_{0}
does not affect how small
T
needs to be). Therefore, by Banach’s fixed point theorem we get that
S
has a unique fixed point
z
. By
[
34
, Lemma 5.12 (a)]

z
is a classical solution of (
3.1
). We can extend the solution further in time by considering the equation with
z(T)
as the initial condition. The fact that we can do it indefinitely follows from (
3.2
), which we now prove. We use the Duhamel formula (
3.4
) in a similar manner as above. For
t\in(0,T)
,
1\leq l\leq k
, and
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
we have (by
(K)
and Remark
2.22
(c))

\displaystyle\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|z_{0}\|_{\infty}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\,ds,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|D^{l}z(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|D^{l}z_{0}\|_{\infty}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{l-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{l-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{l}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,ds,

\displaystyle\|D^{k}z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{k}z_{0}\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\widetilde{C}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\widetilde{C}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1+\sigma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,ds.

By summing up these estimates and using the Grönwall inequality of Lemma
3.2
, we obtain (
3.2
).

(b) The part concerning spatial derivatives is a direct consequence of Lemma
B.2
. Once we have it, continuity for time derivatives follows by using the equation.

(c) This part follows by taking
l=1
in (
3.8
) (note that for
l=1
the estimate also works with
\|Dz(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}
in place of
\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
) and using Lemma
3.2
.
∎

3.2.
Integrable solutions of linear equations with divergence form drifts and sources

Later we will consider equations and systems with data in the negative order Hölder spaces, which we approximate by
L^{1}
functions. The following result gives well-posedness and a basis for compactness arguments for linear equations in the
L^{1}
setting.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that
(K)
holds and let
V_{1}\in(C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d}
,
V_{2}\in(L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, and
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

(a)

There exists a unique
\rho\in C([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfying

(3.9)

\displaystyle\rho(t,x)=K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}(s,y)\rho(s,y)+V_{2}(s,y))\,dy\,ds.

Furthermore,
\rho
is a distributional solution of the equation

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho-\mathcal{L}\rho-\Div(V_{1}\rho)-\Div(V_{2})=0,\quad&{\rm in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\rho(0)=\rho_{0},&{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

when tested with functions
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\partial_{t}\phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see Lemma
C.1
.

(b)

The following estimate holds true:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(T,\|V_{1}\|_{\infty},\underline{\alpha})\bigg(\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\frac{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\alpha}-1}T^{1-1/\underline{\alpha}}\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg)

(c)

There exists
c>0
depending on
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\|V_{1}\|_{\infty},
and
\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},
such that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot+z)-\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho_{0}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+c|z|^{\underline{\alpha}-1}.

(d)

There exists
C>0
depending on
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\|V_{1}\|_{\infty},\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},
and
\rho_{0}
, and a modulus of continuity
\omega
independent of these quantities, such that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)-\rho(s)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\omega(|t-s|),\quad t,s\in[0,T].

Furthermore,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\rho(t)-\rho(s)\|_{C^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(1+\|V_{1}\|_{\infty})+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)|t-s|,\quad t,s\in[0,T].

(e)

Let
\psi
be a tightness measuring function for
\{\rho_{0},\nu\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\}
. Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,|\rho(t,x)|\,dx\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,|\rho_{0}(x)|\,dx+\|D\psi\|_{\infty}\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|D\psi\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big(2+cT\big(\|A\|_{2}+\|B\|_{\infty}+\|b\|_{\infty}+\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\,\nu(dz)\big)+T\int_{|z|\geq 1}\psi(z)\,\nu(dz)\Big).

Proof.

(a) We will establish the existence with the use of Banach’s fixed point theorem in the space
X=L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, continuity in time will be shown in d).
For
\widetilde{\rho}
we let
S(\widetilde{\rho})=\rho
if for every
12
12

12

By Fubini’s theorem
\rho
is measurable in
(t,x)
and in
x
for every fixed
t
.

t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\rho(t,x)

\displaystyle=K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}(s,y)\widetilde{\rho}(s,y)+V_{2}(s,y))\,dy\,ds.

By (
3.15
), Tonelli’s theorem, and
(K)
we get that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(t,x)|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||V_{1}(s,y)\widetilde{\rho}(s,y)+V_{2}(s,y)|\,dx\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\mathcal{K}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{1}(s,y)\widetilde{\rho}(s,y)+V_{2}(s,y)|\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\mathcal{K}T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\frac{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\alpha}-1}\big(\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{\rho}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big).

Therefore we get that
\rho\in L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(and
B([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
). Furthermore, since the map
S
is affine, a similar calculation leads to the fact that for
T
small enough
S
is a contraction on
L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
More explicitly, if

\displaystyle T\leq\bigg(\frac{\underline{\alpha}-1}{2\mathcal{K}\underline{\alpha}\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}}\bigg)^{\frac{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\alpha}-1}},

then
S
is a contraction on
X
. By Banach’s fixed point theorem we get that
S
has a unique fixed point in
X
. Furthermore, since the above choice of
T
does not depend on
\rho_{0}
, we may extend the solution indefinitely, therefore we get the long time existence as well. By Lemma
C.1
we find that
\rho
is a distributional solution of (
3.10
).

(b) In order to get (
3.11
)
we integrate (
3.9
) with respect to
x
and we estimate using triangle inequality, Tonelli’s theorem, and
(K)
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(t,x)|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||V_{1}(s,y)\rho(s,y)+V_{2}(s,y)|\,dx\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(s,y)|\,dy\,ds+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds

\displaystyle=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\frac{\underline{\alpha}}{\underline{\alpha}-1}+\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\rho(s,y)|\,dy\,ds.

By using Lemma
3.2
, we get (
3.11
).

(c) Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,T]
. By Duhamel’s formula (
3.9
),

\displaystyle\|\rho(t,\cdot+z)-\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\ast K_{t}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\ast K_{t}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x+z-y)-D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,ds\,dx.

By Tonelli’s theorem we can estimate the first term as follows:

(3.17)

\displaystyle\|\rho_{0}\ast K_{t}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\ast K_{t}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho_{0}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In the second term we split the time integral into
\int_{0}^{t-\varepsilon}+\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}
. For the first part we use the fundamental theorem of calculus, Tonelli’s theorem and
(K)
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t-\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x+z-y)-D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,dx\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|z|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{t-\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{2}_{yy}K(t-s,x+\lambda z-y)||(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dx\,dy\,ds\,d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|z|\int_{0}^{t-\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|D^{2}_{yy}K(t-s,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|z|\mathcal{K}(\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\int_{0}^{t-\varepsilon}(t-s)^{-\frac{2}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|z|\varepsilon^{1-\frac{2}{\underline{\alpha}}}\cdot C(\underline{\alpha},T)\mathcal{K}(\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}).

The second part is estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x+z-y)-D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,dx\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|D_{y}K(t-s,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{K}(\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\cdot 2C(\underline{\alpha},T)\mathcal{K}(\|V_{1}\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}).

By summing the two estimates, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x+z-y)-D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)|\,dy\,ds\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}(|z|\varepsilon^{1-\frac{2}{\underline{\alpha}}}+\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}})

(3.18)

\displaystyle\leq C|z|^{\underline{\alpha}-1},

where the last inequality was obtained by minimization in
\varepsilon
. Now, (
3.12
) follows by adding (
3.17
) to (
3.18
).

(d) Let
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
0\leq s<t\leq T
. Using the definition of distributional solution and the fact that
\rho
satisfying (
3.9
) is in
B([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x))\varphi(x)\,dx\bigg|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|t-s|\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(1+\|V_{1}\|_{\infty})+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

where
C=C(\mathcal{L}^{*})
.
Note that this gives the second part of the statement. We denote

\displaystyle C_{1}=C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(1+\|V_{1}\|_{\infty})+\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Now let
\phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|\phi\|_{\infty}=1
and let
\eta_{\varepsilon}
be a standard mollifier. We have
\phi_{\varepsilon}:=\phi\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\|\phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x))\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx\bigg|\leq C_{1}|t-s|\varepsilon^{-2}.

Furthermore, if we let
\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x)=g(x)
, then using Fubini’s theorem and a change of variables we get

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\phi(x)\,dx\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(g_{\varepsilon}(x)-g(x))\phi(x)\,dx\bigg|\leq\|\phi\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{|z|\leq\varepsilon}\|g(\cdot+z)-g(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Thus, by using (c) and the fact that
\|\phi\|_{\infty}=1
, we get

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x))\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x))\phi(x)\,dx\bigg|\leq 2\big(\sup\limits_{|z|<\varepsilon}\|\rho_{0}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+c\varepsilon^{\underline{\alpha}-1}\big).

It follows that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho(t,x)-\rho(s,x))\phi(x)\,dx\bigg|\leq C_{1}|t-s|\varepsilon^{-2}+2\big(\sup\limits_{|z|<\varepsilon}\|\rho_{0}(\cdot+z)-\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+c\varepsilon^{\underline{\alpha}-1}\big).

By minimizing in
\varepsilon
(or taking, e.g.,
\varepsilon=|t-s|^{\frac{1}{4}}
) and using the fact that
L^{\infty}
is the dual of
L^{1}
, we get (
3.13
).

(e) The proof consists in testing the equation with
\psi
, but since it is unbounded, an appropriate cut-off argument is needed. We refer to
[
34
, Proposition 6.6 (c)]
for the details.

∎

3.3.
Measure valued solutions of Fokker–Planck equations

Theorem 3.6

(
[
34
, Section 6]
)
.

Assume that
(K)
holds,
b\in(C_{b}((0,T),C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, and that
m_{0}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the density of a probability measure. Then the following assertions hold.

(a)

The equation

(3.19)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}m-\mathcal{L}^{*}m-\Div(bm)=0\quad{\rm in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
m(0)=m_{0}\quad{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

has a unique classical solution
m
. The function
m(t,\cdot)
is the density of a probability measure for every
t\in[0,T]
and

\|m\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}m\|_{\infty}+\|Dm\|_{\infty}+\|D^{2}m\|_{\infty}\leq C,

with
C
depending on
d,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\|m_{0}\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and the constant in
(K)
.

(b)

There exists
c_{0}>0
independent of
m_{0}
such that

(3.20)

d_{0}(m(t),m(s))\leq c_{0}(1+\|b\|_{\infty})|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 3.7
.

(a)

We want to consider general probability measures as the initial conditions in the Fokker–Planck equations. To this end we rework the above result in Theorem
3.9
below. We note that
b\in(C_{b}((0,T),C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
is sufficient for getting mild and distributional solutions, thus the difference between the assumptions in Theorem
3.6
and Theorem
3.9
.

(b)

The Hölder regularity in time is stronger in
[
34
]
than in (
3.20
), because of additional assumption
[
34
, (L2)(i)]
. By inspecting the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 6.6 (b)]
it is easy to see that without this assumption we can always get
\frac{1}{2}
regularity, cf. Remark
2.22
(a).

(c)

The well-posedness and spatial regularity in Theorem
3.6
are proved in
[
34
]
using Duhamel’s principle, much like Lemma
3.3
above. In
[
34
]
the authors assume
(K)
allowing
\underline{\alpha}\in(1,2)
, but in the results that we use here,
\underline{\alpha}=2
can be added without any change in the arguments.

Definition 3.8
.

We say that
m
is a distributional solution of (
3.19
) if for every
\phi\in C_{b}^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
0\leq t\leq T
,

(3.21)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t,x)\,m(t,dx)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(0,x)\,m_{0}(dx)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(\partial_{t}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}\phi(s,x)-D_{x}\phi(s,x)b(s,x)\big)\,m(s,dx)\,ds.

Theorem 3.9
.

Assume that
(K)
holds,
b\in(C_{b}((0,T),C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
and
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the following statements are true.

(a)

The problem (
3.21
) has a distributional solution
m\in C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

(b)

There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
independent of
m_{0}
such that

(3.22)

d_{0}(m(s),m(t))\leq c_{0}(1+\|b\|_{\infty})|s-t|^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad s,t\in[0,T].

(c)

The family
\{m(t):t\in[0,T]\}
is tight and if we let
\psi
be the tightness measuring function from Lemma
2.13
for
m_{0}
and
\nu\textbf{1}_{B_{1}^{c}}
, then there exists
c>0
such that for
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.23)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m(t,dx)\\
\leq&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m_{0}(dx)+\|D\psi\|_{\infty}\bigg(2+cT\big(\|A\|_{2}+\|B\|_{\infty}+\|b\|_{\infty}+\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\,\nu(dz)\big)\bigg)+T\int_{|z|\geq 1}\psi(z)\,\nu(dz).\end{split}

(d)

The distributional solution is unique.

(e)

If the sequence
(b_{n})\subset(C_{b}((0,T),C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
converges to
b
uniformly (i.e.,
\|b_{n}-b\|_{\infty}\to 0
), then the corresponding solutions
m_{n}
converge in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to
m
– the solution corresponding to
b
.

Proof.

(a) The idea is to approximate the solutions for general
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by solutions for regular initial measures obtained in
[
34
]
. Let
\eta
be a standard mollifier and let
m_{0}^{\varepsilon}=m_{0}\ast\eta_{\varepsilon}
. We have
m_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, therefore by the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 6.8 (a)]
(note that only
b,Db\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is used there) there exists
m^{\varepsilon}
which satisfies the Duhamel formula:

(3.24)

m^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K^{*}(t-s,x-y)b(s,y)m^{\varepsilon}(s,y)\,dy\,ds+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K^{*}(t,x-y)\,m_{0}^{\varepsilon}(dy),\quad t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Furthermore,
m^{\varepsilon}(t)\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t\in[0,T]
and
m^{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. By Lemma
C.1

m^{\varepsilon}
is also a distributional solution, therefore by the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 6.6 (a)]
we get that
\int m^{\varepsilon}(t,dx)=1
for each
t\in[0,T]
. Furthermore, if we test the equation with
\phi
satisfying

(3.25)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi-bD_{x}\phi=0,\quad{\rm in}\ [0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\phi(t)=\xi,\end{cases}

with nonnegative
\xi\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see Lemma
3.3
), then by the comparison principle
13
13

13

This is standard, see e.g.
[
34
, Lemma 6.2]
for a similar result. The proof there can easily be adapted to the current setting.
we have
\phi(0)\geq 0
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\xi(x)m^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(0,x)m^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)\,dx\geq 0.

Therefore,
m^{\varepsilon}\geq 0
. Overall, we get
m^{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

We will show that any sequence
(m^{\varepsilon_{n}})
with
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0^{+}
is Cauchy in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Let
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,
t\in(0,T]
,
\phi\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and let
\phi
satisfy (
3.25
). By testing
m^{\varepsilon}-m^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
with
\phi
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\xi(x)(m^{\varepsilon}(t,x)-m^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(t,x))\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(0,x)(m^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)-m^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{0}(x))\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(0)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d_{0}(m^{\varepsilon}_{0},m^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d_{0}(m^{\varepsilon}_{0},m^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}_{0}),

with
C
independent of
t,\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\xi
, see Lemma
3.3
(c). Since by Lemma
2.14
any sequence
(m_{0}^{\varepsilon_{n}})
is Cauchy, Lemma
2.16
gives that any
(m^{\varepsilon_{n}})
is a Cauchy sequence in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Thus, by completeness,
m^{\varepsilon}
converge in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to some
m
. By passing with
\varepsilon
to
0
in (
3.21
) for
m^{\varepsilon}
we conclude that
m
is a distributional solution.

(b) The estimate (
3.22
) is obtained easily from the analogue for
m^{\varepsilon}
in Theorem
3.6
and the triangle inequality:

d_{0}(m(s),m(t))\leq d_{0}(m(s),m^{\varepsilon}(s))+d_{0}(m^{\varepsilon}(s),m^{\varepsilon}(t))+d_{0}(m^{\varepsilon}(t),m(t))\leq C|s-t|^{\frac{1}{2}}+c(\varepsilon),

where
c(\varepsilon)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

(c) The proof of this part is exactly the same as in
[
34
, Proposition 6.6 (c)]
, therefore we skip it (note that only the distributional formulation is used there).

(d) To show the uniqueness, we use a Holmgren-type duality argument. If
m
and
m^{\prime}
are distributional solutions of (
3.19
), then their difference
\widetilde{m}=m-m^{\prime}
is a distributional solution of (
3.19
) with
m_{0}=0
. Let
t\in[0,T]
and assume that
\xi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, by Lemma
3.3
there exists a classical solution to (
3.25
).
By using the above
\phi
in the distributional formulation (
3.21
) we find that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\xi\widetilde{m}(t)=0
. Since
t\in[0,T]
and
\xi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are arbitrary, it follows by Lemma
2.15
that
m=m^{\prime}
.

(e) Let
\widetilde{m}_{n}=m-m_{n}
. Then by subtracting the equations we find that
\widetilde{m}_{n}
satisfies the following problem in the distributional sense:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\widetilde{m}_{n}-\mathcal{L}^{*}\widetilde{m}_{n}-\Div(b\widetilde{m}_{n})=\Div((b_{n}-b)m_{n}),\quad{\rm in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\widetilde{m}_{n}(0)=0.\end{cases}

We take the test function
\phi
satisfying (
3.25
) with
\xi\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|\xi\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
. Then we get

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\xi(x)\,\widetilde{m}_{n}(t,dx)\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi(s,x)(b(s,x)-b_{n}(s,x))\,m_{n}(s,dx)\,ds\bigg|\leq T\|D\phi\|_{\infty}\|b_{n}-b\|_{\infty}.

By Lemma
3.3
(c)
\|D\phi\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C
with
C
independent of
t
and
\xi
which, together with Lemma
2.16
, proves that
m_{n}
converges to
m
in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
∎

3.4.
Smooth solutions of viscous Hamilton–Jacobi equations

The following result shows well-posedness and regularity for viscous Hamilton–Jacobi equations such as the one appearing in the MFG system (
1.1
). The existence and uniqueness part comes directly from
[
34
]
, but the regularity estimates are slightly different. The result here is closer to being optimal, as it uses in full the regularity given by the heat kernel, i.e., we gain
\underline{\alpha}-\varepsilon
derivatives compared to the right-hand side.

Theorem 3.10
.

Assume that
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
, and
(K)
hold. Let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
,
f\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
u_{0}\in C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the Hamilton–Jacobi equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{L}u+H(x,u,Du)=f(t,x)\quad&{\rm in}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(0)=u_{0}\quad&{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

has a unique classical solution
u\in B([0,T],C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([0,T],C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
). Furthermore, there exists
c
, depending only on
d,T
,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\|u_{0}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, and the quantities in the assumptions
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
, and
(K)
, such that

(3.26)

\|\partial_{t}u\|_{\infty}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq c.

Furthermore, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a modulus of continuity independent of
f
and
u_{0}
such that

(3.27)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)-\partial_{t}u(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}+\|u(t,\cdot)-u(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\omega(t-s),\quad s,t\in[0,T].

Sketch of the proof.

The arguments are almost identical to the ones in
[
34
]
, but we present them in order to see that including the case
\underline{\alpha}=2
in
(K)
(cf.
[
34
, (L2)]
) does not change anything in the proof, i.e., the operator can also be local or mixed local-nonlocal. Furthermore, we want
C^{3+\sigma}
regularity for the solution
u
instead of
C^{3}
as in
[
34
]
, which makes the estimates slightly different. Let

\displaystyle X=\{u\in B([0,T],C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq R_{1},\quad u\in C([0,T],C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\},

where
R_{1}
is an explicit constant depending on the constant
\mathcal{K}
in
(K)
,
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, and
\|u_{0}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. Cf. Proposition 5.8 in
[
34
]
(the
f
-dependence of
R_{1}
was overlooked there). We only show how to bound the derivatives for the fixed point map to get a self-map, and we show its contractivity. All the other arguments are the same. Let
\widetilde{u}\in X
and let
u=S(\widetilde{u})
be defined as

\displaystyle u(t,x)=K_{t}\ast u_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)\big(H(y,\widetilde{u}(s,y),D\widetilde{u}(s,y))-f(s,y)\big)\,dy\,ds.

Since
\widetilde{u}\in X
, we can use the dominated convergence theorem (cf. the arguments following (
3.5
)) to find that for
k=1,2,3
we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle D^{k}_{x}u(t,x)=K_{t}\ast D^{k}_{x}u_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)D^{k-1}_{y}\big(H(y,\widetilde{u}(s,y),D\widetilde{u}(s,y))-f(s,y)\big)\,dy\,ds.

Using the regularity of
H
given by
(H1)
14
14

14

Note that the local bounds given there are sufficient, because on
[0,T]
we control
\|u\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
with
u_{0}
and
f
.
and
(K)
we therefore get that

\displaystyle\|D^{k}u(t,\cdot)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{k}_{x}u_{0}\|_{\infty}+\big(C(H)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)|\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{k}_{x}u_{0}\|_{\infty}+C(K,T)\big(C(H)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

with
C(K,T)=C(K)T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
.
Similarly (note that by
(H1)
the second derivatives of
H
are Lipschitz) we get that for any
\widetilde{u_{1}},\widetilde{u_{2}}\in X
,

\displaystyle\|S(\widetilde{u_{1}})(t)-S(\widetilde{u_{2}})(t)\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(K,T,H,R_{1})\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u_{1}}(t,\cdot)-\widetilde{u_{2}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Here,
C(K,T,H,R_{1})
is also small for small
T
. Furthermore, since
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
, by using (
3.28
) and Remark
2.22
(cf. the argument following (
3.7
)) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{|D^{3}u(t,x+h)-D^{3}u(t,x)|}{|h|^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{0}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\big(C(H)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|D_{y}K(t-s,x+h-y)-D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)|}{|h|^{\sigma}}\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{0}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\widetilde{C}(K,T)\big(C(H)\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Here, as before
\widetilde{C}(K,T)=C(K)T^{1-\frac{1+\sigma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\to 0
as
T\to 0^{+}
. Analogously, we get

\displaystyle\|S(\widetilde{u_{1}})(t)-S(\widetilde{u_{2}})(t)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\widetilde{C}(K,T,H,R_{1})\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\widetilde{u_{1}}(t,\cdot)-\widetilde{u_{2}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

with
\widetilde{C}(K,T,H,R_{1})
small for
T
small. This proves that
S
is a contraction if
T
is small enough.
Furthermore, by Lemma
B.2
we get
u\in C([0,T],C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Thus, by taking
T=T_{0}
sufficiently small we get that
S
is a self-map and a contraction. Hence, Banach’s fixed point theorem gives the short-time existence and uniqueness of a mild solution
u
on
[0,T_{0}]
.

Then we prove that
u
is a classical solution which is done exactly as in
[
34
, Section 5]
. Going from the short-time existence to existence on any time interval
[0,T]
is a bit delicate, because we only assume local boundedness for
H
and its derivatives in
u
and
p
, so the constants
c(H)
above actually depend also on
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. It suffices to control this quantity in order to get a lower bound for the length of the short-time interval. This can be done using a result similar to
[
34
, Theorem 5.3 (b), (c)]
and proceeding as in
[
34
, Section 5.2]
. The procedure is rather standard so we skip the details, but the fact that an analogue of
[
34
, Theorem 5.3]
holds in our case is discussed in Remark
3.11
below.

Once we obtain the solution, (
3.26
) follows from the definition of
X
. Then, to get (
3.27
) we use Lemma
B.2
and the original mild formulation of the problem.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

The global
C^{1}_{b}
bound mentioned in the last lines of the proof above is based on the result of Imbert
[
46
, Lemma 2]
. Although
[
46
]
assumes the diffusion to be strictly nonlocal, the proof of
[
46
, Lemma 2]
works for any (linear and translation invariant) operator of the form (
1.2
). Indeed, since our solutions are classical, the extension of the comparison principle
[
46
, Theorem 2]
used in this proof is straightforward.
Then, in order to extend
[
46
, Lemma 2]
to mixed local-nonlocal operators it suffices to use an appropriate viscosity formulation, see e.g. Jakobsen and Karlsen
[
47
]
. Alternatively, since our solutions are very regular, one can also use the Bernstein method, see e.g. Achdou et al.
[
2
, p. 31]
or
[
48
, Theorem 3.3]
for an argument tailored to our case.

4.
Well-posedness of the mean field game system

In this section we prove well-posedness results for the MFG system (
1.1
). These results refine and extend the results in
[
34
]
. For the purpose of the master equation we need to allow probability measures as solutions in the Fokker-Planck equation. We will therefore work in the setting of classical-distributional solutions of (
1.1
). This will allow us to assume less regular data and coefficients than in
[
34
]
. We also show a stronger regularizing effect on
u
than
[
34
]
, without adding any assumptions. Finally we mention that the results of this section seem to be the first ones to consider MFG systems with mixed local-nonlocal diffusion operators.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that the pair
(u,m)
is a solution to the MFG system (
1.1
) if
u\in C^{1,2}_{b}((t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap C([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi equation pointwise and
m\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfies the Fokker–Planck equation in the distributional sense.

Before we proceed to the well-posedness for the MFG system, we give a variant of the Lasry–Lions monotonicity lemma for Hamiltonians
H
which can depend on
u
. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
let
a^{+}=a\vee 0
and
a^{-}=-(a\wedge 0)
and for
m\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
let
m=m^{+}-m^{-}
be its unique Jordan decomposition.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
(M1)
and
(M4)
hold. Let
(u_{1},m_{1})
and
(u_{2},m_{2})
be solutions to the MFG system (
1.1
) on
(t_{0},T)
with initial measures
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}
. Then there exists
C>0
such that the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,u_{2},Du_{1})-H(x,u_{2},Du_{2})-D_{p}H(x,u_{2},Du_{2})(Du_{1}-Du_{2}))\,m_{2}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,u_{1},Du_{2})-H(x,u_{1},Du_{1})-D_{p}H(x,u_{1},Du_{1})(Du_{2}-Du_{1}))\,m_{1}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle C\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u_{1}(t_{0},x)-u_{2}(t_{0},x))^{+}(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx))^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u_{1}(t_{0},x)-u_{2}(t_{0},x))^{-}(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx))^{-}\bigg).

Here and in the proof
u_{i}
and
Du_{i}
are evaluated at
(t,x)
unless specified otherwise.

Proof.

For brevity, let
u=u_{1}-u_{2}
,
m=m_{1}-m_{2}
, and
H_{(i)}=H_{i}(x,u_{i}(t,x),Du_{i}(t,x))
for
i=1,2
. The first part of the proof is standard
[
13
,
56
]
(a detailed computation is given e.g. in
[
34
, Appendix A]
) and consists in using the equations satisfied by
u_{i}
and
m_{i}
and the monotonicity assumption for
F
in
(M1)
to prove that for
t\in(t_{0},T)
,

(4.1)

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(t,x)\,m(t,dx)\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(1)}-H_{(2)}-D_{p}H_{(2)}\cdot Du)\,m_{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(2)}-H_{(1)}-D_{p}H_{(1)}\cdot(-Du))\,m_{1}.

We remark here that the approach in
[
13
, Lemma 3.1.2]
of approximating
m_{0}^{i}
by measures with smooth densities, leads to a circular reasoning because it requires the stability of the MFG system. Instead, one should compute the above derivative using difference quotients and the distributional formulation for the Fokker–Planck equation.

By integrating (
4.1
) with respect to
t
and by using the monotonicity of
G
in
(M1)
we further get that for
s\in[t_{0},T]
,

(4.2)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\geq&\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(1)}-H_{(2)}-D_{p}H_{(2)}\cdot Du)\,m_{2}(t)\,dt\\
&+\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(2)}-H_{(1)}-D_{p}H_{(1)}\cdot(-Du))\,m_{1}(t)\,dt.\end{split}

We add the following term to both sides of (
4.2
):

\displaystyle I(s):=-\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(1)}-H(x,u_{2},Du_{1}))\,m_{2}(t)\,dt-\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{(2)}-H(x,u_{1},Du_{2}))\,m_{1}(t)\,dt.

The right-hand side of (
4.2
) then becomes

\displaystyle J(s):=

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,u_{2},Du_{1})-H(x,u_{2},Du_{2})-D_{p}H(x,u_{2},Du_{2})(Du_{1}-Du_{2}))\,m_{2}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,u_{1},Du_{2})-H(x,u_{1},Du_{1})-D_{p}H(x,u_{1},Du_{1})(Du_{2}-Du_{1}))\,m_{1}(t)\,dt,

so (
4.2
) can be rewritten as

(4.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(s,x)\,m(s,dx)+I(s)\geq J(s),\quad s\in[t_{0},T].

By
(M4)
we have
H(x,u,p)=H_{1}(x,p)+H_{2}(x,u)
, thus

\displaystyle I(s)=

\displaystyle-\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))\,m_{2}(t,dx)\,dt-\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{2})-H_{2}(x,u_{1}))\,m_{1}(t,dx)\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))\,m(t,dx)\,dt.

Note that

\displaystyle I(s)=\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))\,m(t,dx)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))^{+}\,m(t,dx)^{+}\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))^{-}\,m(t,dx)^{-}\,dt,

By the mean value theorem and
(M4)
we have

\displaystyle(H_{2}(x,u_{1})-H_{2}(x,u_{2}))^{\pm}=(D_{u}H_{2}(x,\xi)(u_{1}-u_{2}))^{\pm}=D_{u}H_{2}(x,\xi)((u_{1}-u_{2}))^{\pm}\leq c_{2}(u_{1}-u_{2})^{\pm}=c_{2}u^{\pm}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle I(s)\leq c_{2}\bigg(\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(t,x)^{+}\,m(t,dx)^{+}\,dt+\int_{s}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(t,x)^{-}\,m(t,dx)^{-}\,dt\bigg).

Furthermore,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(s,x)^{+}\,m(s,dx)^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(s,x)^{-}\,m(s,dx)^{-},

therefore (
4.3
) yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{+}m^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{-}m^{-}+c_{2}\int_{s}^{T}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{+}m^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u^{-}m^{-}\bigg)\geq J(s),\quad s\in[t_{0},T].

By Grönwall’s inequality there exists
C>0
such that

C\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(t_{0},x)^{+}\,m(t_{0},x)^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(t_{0},x)^{-}\,m(t_{0},dx)^{-}\bigg)\geq J(t_{0}),

which ends the proof.
∎

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\sigma)
, and assume that
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
,
(K)
,
(F1)
, and
(G1(

σ

))
hold. Then,

(a)

the system (
1.1
) has a solution
(u,m)
such that
u\in B([t_{0},T],C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([t_{0},T],C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
m\in C^{\frac{1}{2}}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(d,T,F,G,H,\mathcal{L},\sigma),

\displaystyle d_{0}(m(t),m(s))\leq C(d,T,F,G,H,\mathcal{L})|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad t,s\in[t_{0},T].

In particular, the constants do not depend on
t_{0}
and
m_{0}
.

(b)

If in addition
(M1)
and
(M4)
are true, then the solution is unique.

Proof.

(a) The proof uses a Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point argument very similar to the one in
[
34
, Theorem 3.2]
(see also
[
2
,
56
]
), so we will be brief. Let
\psi
be the tightness measuring function of Lemma
2.13
for
m_{0}
and
\nu\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}
. We define

X=\bigg\{\mu\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):\mu(t_{0})=m_{0},\,\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,\mu(t,dx)\leq C_{1},\,\sup\limits_{s\neq t}\frac{d_{0}(\mu(s),\mu(t))}{|s-t|^{1/2}}\leq C_{2}\bigg\},

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
will be specified later. By standard arguments including the Prokhorov and Arzelà–Ascoli theorems we get that
X
is convex and compact in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

For
\mu\in X
we let
u\in B([t_{0},T],C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([t_{0},T],C^{3+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be the unique classical solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation of (
1.1
) with
\mu
in place of
m
. The solution exists by virtue of Theorem
3.10
, which we can use because of
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
,
(K)
,
(F1)
,
(G1(

σ

))
, and the fact that
\mu\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. We also get that for
b=D_{p}H(x,u,Du)
we have
\|b\|_{\infty}\leq\widetilde{C}
, with
\widetilde{C}
independent of
\mu
. We then let
m
be the unique distributional solution of the Fokker–Planck equation of (
1.1
) with the function
u
obtained above, see Theorem
3.9
. Next we define the mapping
S\colon X\to C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as
T(\mu)=m
. By taking
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
to be the right-hand sides of estimates (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) respectively, with
\|b\|_{\infty}
replaced by
\widetilde{C}
(as was done in
[
34
]
), we ensure that
S\colon X\to X
.

In order to get the continuity of
S
, let
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Using
(F1)
and
(G1(

σ

))
, as in
[
34
]
we get that the corresponding solutions of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation
u_{n}
, with
Du_{n},D^{2}u_{n},\partial_{t}u_{n},
and
\mathcal{L}u_{n}
converge uniformly to
u,Du,D^{2}u,\partial_{t}u
, and
\mathcal{L}u
respectively, and
u
solves the H–J equation with
\mu
in place of
m
. Now, let
m_{n}
and
m
be the solutions of the Fokker–Planck equation with
u_{n}
and
u
respectively. Then the convergence
m_{n}\to m
in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
follows by taking
b_{n}(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,u_{n}(t,x),Du_{n}(t,x))
and
b(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,u(t,x),Du(t,x))
in Theorem
3.9
(e).

By the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem
S
has a fixed point in
X
, from which we conclude that the MFG system (
1.1
) has a solution. The regularity properties of
m
and
u
follow from respectively, the construction of the solution (the definition of
X
) and Theorem
3.10
, see also Remark
3.1
.

(b) Assume that
(u_{1},m_{1})
and
(u_{2},m_{2})
solve the MFG system with the same data. We use Lemma
4.2
: by the strict convexity in
p
for
\widetilde{H}
in
(M4)
we get that

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}(t,x)-Du_{2}(t,x)|^{2}(m_{1}(t,dx)+m_{2}(t,dx))=0,

therefore for almost every
t
we have
Du_{1}(t)=Du_{2}(t)
both
m_{1}(t)
- and
m_{2}(t)
-almost everywhere. Since
D_{p}H
depends only on
x
and
Du
, we find that
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
solve exactly the same Fokker–Planck equation, therefore by the uniqueness in Theorem
3.9
we obtain
m_{1}=m_{2}
. From this we get that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
solve the same Hamilton–Jacobi equation, therefore by Theorem
3.10
they are equal as well, which proves the uniqueness for the whole system.
∎

5.
Further regularity for the MFG system

The proof of the well-posedness for the master equation requires further regularity and stability results for the solutions of the MFG system. In particular we need to establish that the dependence of
u
on the initial measure
m_{0}
is not only continuous, but also differentiable. This section is devoted to proving these properties. The structure of the results and some of the proofs follow
[
13
, Chapter 3]
.
The results obtained below will imply differentiability properties for the following function, which turns out to be a solution to the master equation.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(u,m)
solve (
1.1
) with
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given. We define

(5.1)

U(t_{0},x,m_{0})=u(t_{0},x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Remark 5.2
.

We have
\mathcal{L}_{x}U(t_{0},x,m_{0})=\mathcal{L}_{x}u(t_{0},x)
and
\partial^{\alpha}_{x}U(t_{0},x,m_{0})=\partial^{\alpha}_{x}u(t_{0},x)
for any multi-index
\alpha
for which the right-hand side makes sense.

5.1.
Lipschitz continuity in the measure variable

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
and assume
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
,
(K)
,
(F1)
,
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
,
(G1(

σ

))
,
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
,
(M1)
, and
(M3)
. Let
(u_{1},m_{1})
and
(u_{2},m_{2})
be the solutions to the MFG system (
1.1
) on
(t_{0},T)
with initial measures
m_{0}^{1}
and
m_{0}^{2}
respectively. Then there exists
C>0
independent of
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}
such that

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\bigg(d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t))+\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg)\leq Cd_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

As a consequence,

\|U(t,\cdot,m)-U(t,\cdot,m^{\prime})\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Cd_{0}(m,m^{\prime}).

Remark 5.4
.

Assumptions
(M1)
,
(M3)
yield the standard Lasry–Lions monotonicity estimate (see
[
34
, Appendix A]
and
[
13
, Lemma 3.1.2]
):

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,Du_{1})-H(x,Du_{2})-D_{p}H(x,Du_{2})(Du_{1}-Du_{2}))\,m_{2}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(H(x,Du_{2})-H(x,Du_{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du_{1})(Du_{2}-Du_{1}))\,m_{1}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u_{1}(t_{0},x)-u_{2}(t_{0},x))(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx)).

The monotonicity condition in Lemma
4.2
which allows
u
-depending Hamiltonians
H
, despite being sufficient for uniqueness arguments for the MFG system (
1.1
), does not seem strong enough to give stability results like Theorem
5.3
that are needed for the analysis of the master equation (
1.3
). The reason is that
an estimate in terms of
d_{0}((m_{0}^{1}-m_{0}^{2})^{+},0)
and
d_{0}((m_{0}^{1}-m_{0}^{2})^{-},0)
given by Lemma
4.2
, is in general not enough to get the estimate in terms of
d_{0}(m_{0}^{1}-m_{0}^{2},0)=d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})
in Theorem
5.3
.
Therefore we drop the
u
-dependence in
H
and assume
(M3)
in place of
(M4)
. Hence in what follows we have
H=H(x,Du)
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

1) We first establish a structure estimate. By using the Lasry–Lions condition of Remark
5.4
,
(M3)
, and the definition of
d_{0}
, we find that

(5.2)

\begin{split}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}(t,x)-Du_{2}(t,x)|^{2}(m_{1}(t,dx)+m_{2}(t,dx))\,dt\leq\,&C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(u_{1}(t_{0},x)-u_{2}(t_{0},x))(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx))\\
\leq\,&C\|u_{1}(t_{0},\cdot)-u_{2}(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).\end{split}

2) We will now estimate
d_{0}(m_{1},m_{2})
. By assumption,
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
satisfy the Fokker–Planck equation in the distributional sense (
3.21
), thus for every
\phi\in C^{1,2}_{b}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t,x)(m_{1}(t,dx)-m_{2}(t,dx))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t_{0},x)(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx))

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(\partial_{t}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}\phi(s,x)-D_{x}\phi(s,x)D_{p}H(x,Du_{1})\big)(m_{1}(s,dx)-m_{2}(s,dx))\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\phi(s,x)(D_{p}H(x,Du_{2})-D_{p}H(x,Du_{1}))\,m_{2}(s,dx)\,ds.

Fix an arbitrary
\phi_{0}\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}){}\cap C^{4}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\phi
be the solution of the linear equation (
3.1
) on
(t_{0},t)
with
\phi(t)=\phi_{0}
,
b(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,Du_{1})
, and
f=0
, which exists by Lemma
3.3
and the fact that
Du_{1}
has two continuous and bounded spatial derivatives (see Theorem
4.3
). Then the middle integral above vanishes, and by
(H1)
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{0}(x)(m_{1}(t,dx)-m_{2}(t,dx))\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t_{0},x)(m_{0}^{1}(dx)-m_{0}^{2}(dx))\bigg|+\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\phi(s,x)(D_{p}H(x,Du_{2})-D_{p}H(x,Du_{1}))\,m_{2}(s,dx)\,ds\bigg|

(5.3)

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle C\bigg(d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}-Du_{2}|\,m_{2}(s,dx)\,ds\bigg).

Note that by (
3.3
) there is
C>0
independent of
\phi_{0}
such that
\sup_{s\in[t_{0},t]}\|\phi(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\phi_{0}\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2C
.
By Jensen’s inequality and (
5.2
) we can estimate the last term as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}-Du_{2}|\,m_{2}(s,dx)\,ds

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}-Du_{2}|\,m_{2}(s,dx)\,ds\bigg)^{2\cdot\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(T-t_{0})\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Du_{1}-Du_{2}|^{2}(m_{1}(s,dx)+m_{2}(s,dx))\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|u_{1}(t_{0},\cdot)-u_{2}(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By this and (
5.3
) it follows that

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{0}(x)(m_{1}(t,dx)-m_{2}(t,dx))\bigg|\leq C\bigg(d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg),

so by taking the supremum over
\phi_{0}\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}){}\cap C^{4}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see Lemma
2.16
) and
t
, we obtain

(5.4)

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t))\leq C\bigg(d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

3) Now consider
w=u_{1}-u_{2}
. By the Hamilton–Jacobi equations for
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
,

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}w+\mathcal{L}w+b(t,x)\cdot Dw=f(t,x)\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
w(T)=w_{T}\quad&{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle b(t,x)=\int_{0}^{1}D_{p}H(x,\lambda Du_{2}+(1-\lambda)Du_{1})\,d\lambda,

\displaystyle f(t,x)=F(x,m_{1}(t))-F(x,m_{2}(t))=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m_{1}(t)+(1-\lambda)m_{2}(t),y)(m_{2}(t,dy)-m_{1}(t,dy))\,d\lambda,\ {\rm and}

\displaystyle w_{T}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m_{1}(T)+(1-\lambda)m_{2}(T),y)(m_{2}(T,dy)-m_{1}(T,dy))\,d\lambda.

By the bounds on
\|u\|_{C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
in Theorem
4.3
and by
(H1)
there exists
C=C(d,T,F,G,H,\mathcal{L})
such that

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|b(t,x)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C.

Furthermore, by
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
and
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
respectively, we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(F)\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t)),\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\|w_{T}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(G)d_{0}(m_{1}(T),m_{2}(T))\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t)).

Therefore, by Lemma
3.3
we obtain

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|w(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t)),

where
C=C(d,T,F,G,H,\mathcal{L})
. Thus, by (
5.4
),

(5.5)

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\bigg(d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

By using
ab\leq\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{C}a^{2}+Cb^{2})
and rearranging we get

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u_{1}(t,\cdot)-u_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Cd_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

By inserting this estimate into (
5.4
) we get

\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{1}(t),m_{2}(t))\leq Cd_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

which ends the proof.
∎

5.2.
Estimates on an auxiliary forward-backward linear system

In order to obtain existence of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
and its regularity in
m_{0}
and
t_{0}
, we will study several variants of linearized systems coming from the MFG system (
1.1
). To cover all cases we consider a quite general linear forward-backward system in (
5.7
) below.

Let
V\colon(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
b\colon(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
z_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
m\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\rho_{0}\in C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
c\in(L^{1}([t_{0},T],C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
for some
k\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
and
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
. Assume also that
\Gamma\colon(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\Gamma(t,x)
is a symmetric matrix for every
(t,x)
. Furthermore, we stipulate that there exists
c_{\Gamma}>0
such that for every
(t,x)
,

(5.6)

\displaystyle c_{\Gamma}^{-1}Id\leq\Gamma(t,x)\leq c_{\Gamma}Id,

the inequality
A\leq B
meaning that
B-A
is non-negative definite, i.e.,
((B-A)x,x)\geq 0
.
Consider the following forward-backward linear system:

(5.7)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}z-\mathcal{L}z+V(t,x)\cdot Dz=\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m(t)),\rho(t)\rangle+b(t,x)\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\partial_{t}\rho-\mathcal{L}^{*}\rho-\Div(\rho V)-\Div(m\Gamma Dz+c)=0\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
z(T,x)=\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m(T)),\rho(T)\rangle+z_{T}(x),\quad\rho(t_{0})=\rho_{0}.\end{cases}

Here and below we use the expression
\langle\phi,\rho\rangle
to denote the functional
\rho\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
acting on
\phi\in C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\gamma>0
. We say that the pair
(z,\rho)
is a classical solution of (
5.7
) if
z\in C([t_{0},T],C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
solves the first equation pointwise and
\rho\in C_{b}((t_{0},T),C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
solves the second equation in very weak sense, that is,
\rho(t_{0})=\rho_{0}
and for every
t\in(t_{0},T)
and
\phi
such that
\phi(t_{0}),\phi(t)\in C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\phi
,
\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi-VD\phi\in L^{\infty}((t_{0},t),C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, it satisfies

(5.8)

\begin{split}&\langle\phi(t),\rho(t)\rangle-\langle\phi(t_{0}),\rho_{0}\rangle\\
=&\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle(\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi-VD\phi)(s),\rho(s)\rangle\,ds-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi\Gamma Dz(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle D\phi(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds.\end{split}

With appropriate data, the first equation of (
5.7
) corresponds to
\langle\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m},\rho\rangle
, as we will show in Subsection
5.3
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
V,\Gamma,b,z_{T},m,c
, and
\rho_{0}
be defined as above, let
k\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
,
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
, and assume that
(K)
,
(M2)
,
(F2(k,

σ

))
, and
(G2(k,

σ

))
hold. Suppose in addition that
\Gamma\in(C([t_{0},T],C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d\times d}
satisfies (
5.6
),
V\in(L^{\infty}([t_{0},T],C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}\cap(C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
,
c\in(L^{1}([t_{0},T],C^{-k-\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}\cap(C([t_{0},T],\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
15
15

15

Recall that
\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of measure representable functionals in
C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
for some
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
and small
\varepsilon>0
,
b\in L^{\infty}([t_{0},T],C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\rho_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
z_{T}\in C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then (
5.7
) has a unique classical solution
(z,\rho)
such that for every
\delta\in(0,\sigma)
we have
z\in B([t_{0},T],C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([t_{0},T],C^{k+2+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\rho\in C([t_{0},T],C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore, if we let

\displaystyle M=\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))},

where
\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|b(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, then the following estimate holds true:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq CM,

where
C=C(d,T,\underline{\alpha},V,c_{\Gamma},F,G)
.

Remark 5.6
.

It is very likely that the result can be done for
k=0
. The limitation
k\geq 1
appears because we use the classical solution setting of Lemma
3.3
for the test function
w
in (
5.18
) below.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

We first prove the result for
\rho_{0}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
m\in C([t_{0},T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
c\in(C([t_{0},T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, using the Leray–Schauder theorem
[
38
, Theorem 10.3]
. To this end we establish a compact fixed point map and we obtain appropriate a priori estimates for the system. After solving the system for regular data, we handle the general data by convolving it with
\eta_{\varepsilon}
and we show that the solutions for the regularized problem converge to the solution for general data. Note that by Lemmas
2.7
and
2.12
, the convolved data has the regularity listed above (here we use the assumption
c\in(C([t_{0},T],\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
). We also recall from Section
2.1
that
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is embedded in
C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
\gamma>0
with the convention
\langle f,\varphi\rangle=\int f\varphi
for
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and we have
\|\cdot\|_{C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

1)
The fixed point map
T
.
Let

\displaystyle X=C([t_{0},T],C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

For every
\widetilde{\rho}\in X
, we want to define
\rho=T(\tilde{\rho})\in X
in the way that the fixed point of
T
solves the linear system (
5.7
). In order to do this, we first solve the equation for
z
with
\widetilde{\rho}
in place of
\rho
.
Recall that
V\in(C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
and
b\in C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore, by
(F2(k,

σ

))
,
(G2(k,

σ

))
and Lemma
2.8
, we get that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

\displaystyle\bigg\|\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t)),\widetilde{\rho}(t)\bigg\rangle\bigg\|_{C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\sup\limits_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg\|\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\widetilde{\rho}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\widetilde{\rho}\|_{X},

(5.11)

\displaystyle\bigg\|\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(T)),\widetilde{\rho}(T)\bigg\rangle\bigg\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\sup\limits_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg\|\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m,\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\|\widetilde{\rho}(T)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\widetilde{\rho}\|_{X}.

Finally, since
m\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we find that
\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t)),\widetilde{\rho}(t)\rangle\in C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Thus, by Lemma
3.3
we get that there exists a classical solution
z
to the first equation in (
5.7
) with
\widetilde{\rho}
in place of
\rho
, and we have

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\widetilde{\rho}\|_{X}+\|b\|_{k+1}).

We now set
V_{1}=V
and
V_{2}=m\Gamma Dz+c
, and we note that
V_{1}\in(C_{b}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d}
and
V_{2}\in(C([t_{0},T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
with

(5.12)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|V_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma\|_{\infty}\|Dz\|_{\infty}+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|c(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\widetilde{\rho}\|_{X}+\|b\|_{k+1})+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|c(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Therefore, by Lemma
3.5
(see also Lemma
C.1
), we get the existence of
\rho\in C([t_{0},T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subset X
solving in the very weak sense the second equation of (
5.7
) with
z
obtained above. We choose the only such
\rho
which satisfies the Duhamel formula (
3.9
) and define
T(\widetilde{\rho})=\rho
.

2)
Continuity and compactness of
T
.
Since the equations are linear, it is easy to check that
T
is continuous: consider a sequence
\widetilde{\rho}_{n}
converging to
\widetilde{\rho}
in
X
. By (
5.10
), (
5.11
) and (
3.2
) we get that for the corresponding solutions
z_{n}
and
z
we have
\|Dz_{n}-Dz\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then, by (
5.12
) and (
3.11
) we find that the corresponding
\rho_{n}
converge to
\rho
in
C([t_{0},T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, hence also in
X
.

In order to get that
T
maps any bounded set
B\subseteq X
to a pre-compact subset of
X
, we use the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem. By Lemma
3.5
(d) we get equicontinuity in
t
for
T(B)
. To get pre-compactness in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(and so in
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) for every fixed
t
, we use the Kolmogorov–Riesz theorem
[
43
, Theorem 5]
: Lemma
3.5
(b),(c), and (e) give boundedness, continuity of translations, and tightness of
\{T(\widetilde{\rho})(t):\widetilde{\rho}\in B\}
respectively, so all the assumptions of the Kolmogorov–Riesz theorem are satisfied. It yields that
\{T(\widetilde{\rho})(t):\widetilde{\rho}\in B\}
is pre-compact in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
. Thus we are in a position to use the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, which yields that
T
is a compact map.

3)
The set
\mathcal{FP}_{\lambda}:=\{\rho\in X:\exists\lambda\in[0,1]\ \rho=\lambda T(\rho)\}
appearing in the Leray–Schauder theorem is bounded and (
5.9
) holds.

First note that
0\in\mathcal{FP}_{\lambda}
so the set is nonempty. Next, if
\rho_{\lambda}\in X
satisfies
\rho_{\lambda}=\lambda T(\rho_{\lambda})
for some
\lambda\in[0,1]
, then there is
z_{\lambda}
such that
(z_{\lambda},\rho_{\lambda})
solves the following system:

(5.14)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}z_{\lambda}-\mathcal{L}z_{\lambda}+V(t,x)\cdot Dz_{\lambda}=\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m(t)),\rho_{\lambda}(t)\rangle+b(t,x)\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{\lambda}-\mathcal{L}^{*}\rho_{\lambda}-\Div(\rho_{\lambda}V)-\lambda\Div(m\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}+c)=0\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
z_{\lambda}(T,x)=\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m(T)),\rho_{\lambda}(T)\rangle+z_{T}(x),\quad\rho_{\lambda}(t_{0})=\lambda\rho_{0}.\end{cases}

We will prove that
\mathcal{FP}_{\lambda}
is bounded in
X
by showing that the a priori estimate (
5.9
) holds for any solution of (
5.14
), independently of
\lambda
. We emphasize that the arguments below do not use the smoothness of
\rho_{0},m,
and
c
.

We start by establishing a structure estimate for solutions of (
5.14
). By using
z_{\lambda}
as a test function in
\rho_{\lambda}
-equation and then using the
z_{\lambda}
-equation, we get

\displaystyle\langle z_{\lambda}(T),\rho_{\lambda}(T)\rangle-\lambda\langle z_{\lambda}(t_{0}),\rho_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\langle(\partial_{t}z_{\lambda}+\mathcal{L}z_{\lambda}-VDz_{\lambda})(s),\rho_{\lambda}(s)\rangle\,ds-\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds-\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\langle Dz_{\lambda}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(m(s)),\rho_{\lambda}(s)\big\rangle_{y}+b(s),\,\rho_{\lambda}(s)\bigg\rangle_{x}\,ds-\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds-\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\langle Dz_{\lambda}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds.

By rearranging and using the terminal condition for
z_{\lambda}
we get

(5.15)

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds

\displaystyle=\lambda\langle z_{\lambda}(t_{0}),\rho_{0}\rangle-\langle z_{T},\rho_{\lambda}(T)\rangle-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\langle b(s),\rho_{\lambda}(s)\rangle\,ds-\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\langle Dz_{\lambda}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds

(5.16)

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(m(s)),\rho_{\lambda}(s)\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho_{\lambda}(s)\bigg\rangle_{x}\,ds-\bigg\langle\big\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(m(T)),\rho_{\lambda}(T)\big\rangle_{y},\,\rho_{\lambda}(T)\bigg\rangle_{x}.

By
(M2)
the last two terms are negative, therefore we can estimate the left-hand side as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\leq

\displaystyle\,\lambda|\langle z_{\lambda}(t_{0}),\rho_{0}\rangle|+|\langle z_{T},\rho_{\lambda}(T)\rangle|+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}|\langle b(s),\rho_{\lambda}(s)\rangle|\,ds+\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{T}|\langle Dz_{\lambda}(s),c(s)\rangle|\,ds

(5.17)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\lambda\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+T\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}).

Now we will estimate
\rho_{\lambda}
by duality. Let
\xi\in C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\tau\in(t_{0},T]
, and let
w
be the unique solution to the problem

(5.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w(t,x)+\mathcal{L}w(t,x)-V(t,x)Dw(t,x)=0,\ {\rm in}\ (t_{0},\tau)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad w(\tau)=\xi.

Existence and uniqueness of
w
is given by Lemma
3.3
. We also get that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|w(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

with
C
depending on
\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|V(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, but not on
\xi
. Therefore, by testing the
\rho_{\lambda}
-equation in (
5.14
) with
w
and using the triangle inequality we get

\displaystyle|\langle\xi,\rho_{\lambda}(\tau)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda|\langle w(t_{0}),\rho_{0}\rangle|+\lambda\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dw\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg|+\lambda\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau}\langle Dw(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))})+\lambda\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Dw\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)|\,m(s,dx)\,ds.

Since
\Gamma
is symmetric and non-negative definite, we have

\displaystyle|Dw\cdot\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}|=|\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}Dw\cdot\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}Dz_{\lambda}|\leq|\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}Dw|^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}Dz_{\lambda}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq(\Gamma Dw\cdot Dw)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}\cdot Dz_{\lambda})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the fact that
\Gamma\leq CId
, and the bounds on
Dw
we get

\displaystyle|\langle\xi,\rho_{\lambda}(\tau)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))})

\displaystyle+\lambda\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dw\Gamma Dw(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg(\lambda\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lambda\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\lambda\bigg(\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Dz_{\lambda}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg).

Now, by estimate (
5.17
) and the subadditivity of the square root we get

\displaystyle|\langle\xi,\rho_{\lambda}(\tau)\rangle|\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big[

\displaystyle\lambda\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lambda\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle+\lambda\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle+\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}+T\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}})\big].

If we divide by
\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and take the supremum over
\tau\in[t_{0},T]
and
\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
, we find that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\big[

\displaystyle\lambda\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lambda\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle+\lambda\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle+\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}+T\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}})\big].

We use
ab\leq\frac{1}{2}(ca^{2}+\frac{1}{c}b^{2})
in the last two terms, with
c=\frac{1}{C}
for the
\rho_{\lambda}
term and small
c
to be determined later for the
z_{\lambda}
term. After rearranging and using the definition of
M
we obtain

(5.19)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\lambda\big[M+c`\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big].

Note that
\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
can be estimated in the exact same way as in step 1), by using
\rho_{\lambda}
instead of
\widetilde{\rho}
in (
5.10
) and (
5.11
). This means that we have

(5.20)
