\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(\|z_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|b\|_{k+1+\sigma}).

From this and (
5.19
), by taking
c
sufficiently small and rearranging we obtain

(5.21)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{\lambda}(t)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\lambda M\leq CM.

Then, (
5.20
) yields an analogous estimate for
z_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(1+\lambda)M\leq 2CM.

In particular, we get (
5.9
) for
(z_{\lambda},\rho_{\lambda})
independently of
\lambda
, and hence also the set
\mathcal{FP_{\lambda}}
is bounded in
X
.

4)
Existence for regular data by Leray-Schauder fixed point theorem.
By step 2)
T
is a continuous and compact map on
X
, and by step 3) the set
\mathcal{FP}_{\lambda}=\{\rho\in X:\exists\lambda\in[0,1]\ \rho=\lambda T(\rho)\}
is bounded in
X
. Therefore, by the Leray–Schauder theorem we get the existence of a fixed point of
T
.

5)
Existence for general data by approximation.
We will now resolve the case of general
m
,
c
, and
\rho_{0}
satisfying the assumptions of the theorem. Let
m_{n}=m\ast\eta_{\frac{1}{n}}
and
c_{n}=c\ast\eta_{\frac{1}{n}}
,
\rho_{0}^{n}=\rho_{0}\ast\eta_{\frac{1}{n}}
be their approximating sequences. By Lemmas
2.3
and
2.14
they converge to
m,c
, and
\rho_{0}
in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
(L^{1}([t_{0},T],C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, and
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
norms respectively as
n\to\infty
. For natural numbers
n\neq n^{\prime}
the pair
(z_{n,n^{\prime}},\rho_{n,n^{\prime}}):=(z_{n}-z_{n^{\prime}},\rho_{n}-\rho_{n^{\prime}})
solves the system (
5.7
) with
m=m_{n}
and

(5.22)

\begin{split}b_{n,n^{\prime}}(t,x)&=\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m_{n}(t)),\rho_{n^{\prime}}(t)\bigg\rangle-\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m_{n^{\prime}}(t)),\rho_{n^{\prime}}(t)\bigg\rangle,\\
z_{T}^{n,n^{\prime}}(x)&=\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m_{n}(T)),\rho_{n^{\prime}}(T)\bigg\rangle-\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m_{n^{\prime}}(T)),\rho_{n^{\prime}}(T)\bigg\rangle,\\
c_{n,n^{\prime}}(t,x)&=(m_{n^{\prime}}-m_{n})\Gamma(t,x)Dz_{n^{\prime}}(t,x)+c_{n}-c_{n^{\prime}},\\
\rho_{0}^{n,n^{\prime}}&=\rho_{0}^{n}-\rho_{0}^{n^{\prime}}.\end{split}

By
(F2(k,

σ

))
and
(G2(k,

σ

))
we get that

\displaystyle\|b_{n,n^{\prime}}\|_{k+1+\sigma}+\|z^{n,n^{\prime}}_{T}\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq(C(F)+C(G))\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n}(t),m_{n^{\prime}}(t))\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Furthermore, the first term in
c_{n,n^{\prime}}
can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\|(m_{n^{\prime}}-m_{n})\Gamma Dz_{n^{\prime}}\|_{-k-\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\|(m_{n^{\prime}}-m_{n})\Gamma Dz_{n^{\prime}}\|_{-1}\quad

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[t_{0},T]\\
\|\phi\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1\end{subarray}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Gamma(t,x)Dz_{n^{\prime}}(t,x)\phi(x)(m_{n^{\prime}}(dx)-m_{n}(dx))\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\Gamma(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n^{\prime}}(t),m_{n}(t)).

Therefore, by (
5.9
) we find that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\big(\|z_{n,n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\rho_{n,n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big[(C(F)+C(G))\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n}(t),m_{n^{\prime}}(t))\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\Gamma(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z_{n^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n^{\prime}}(t),m_{n}(t))

\displaystyle+\|c_{n}-c_{n^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\|\rho_{0}^{n}-\rho_{0}^{n^{\prime}}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big],

where
C
does not depend on
n,n^{\prime}
, and
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, by (
5.9
) the bounds on
\rho_{n^{\prime}}
and
z_{n^{\prime}}
are uniform in
n^{\prime}
, therefore we get that
(z_{n},\rho_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence in
C([t_{0},T],C^{k+2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C([t_{0},T],C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and so, it converges to some
(z,\rho)
. It is easy to see that it solves the system (
5.7
) and satisfies estimates (
5.9
).

6) The uniqueness follows by noting that if pairs
(z_{1},\rho_{1})
and
(z_{2},\rho_{2})
solve (
5.7
), then their difference solves (
5.7
) with
b,c,z_{T}
, and
\rho_{0}
equal to
0
. Thus, by estimate (
5.9
) and the definition of
M
, we get that they are equal.
∎

We can get improved space regularity for
\rho
in the linear system if we allow the norms to blow up at the initial time.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume that
(K)
holds,
V_{1}\in(C([0,T],C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
,
V_{2}\in(C([0,T],(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{0}))\cap B([0,T],\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, and
\rho_{0}\in C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
n\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Then the problem

(5.23)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho-\mathcal{L}\rho-\Div(\rho V_{1})-\Div(V_{2})=0,\quad{\rm on}\ (0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\rho(0)=\rho_{0}.\end{cases}

has a distributional solution
\rho
(in the sense of Lemma
C.2
) such that
\rho\in C((0,T],C^{\gamma-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap B([0,T],C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for every
\gamma\in(0,\underline{\alpha})
and

(5.24)

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in(0,T]}\|t^{\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\rho(t)\|_{C^{\gamma-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(V_{1})(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}).

If we assume that
\rho_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e., it is measure representable (see Definition
2.5
), then
\rho(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We will use Duhamel’s formula and Banach’s fixed point theorem. We only discuss the case
\rho_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is slightly more subtle, because aside from the estimates it also requires to verify if being measure representable is preserved under the fixed point map. Fix
\gamma\in(0,\underline{\alpha})
, let
M=2C_{1}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV})
, where
C_{1}
is the constant in (
5.27
) below, and define
16
16

16

For
\rho_{0}\in C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
simply replace
C_{b}((0,T],\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by
C_{b}((0,T],C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
in the definition of
X
and follow the proof.

\displaystyle X=\big\{\rho\in C_{b}((0,T],\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C((0,T],C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):\sup\limits_{t\in(0,T]}\|t^{\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\rho(t)\|_{C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq M\big\},

with metric induced by the norm
\|\rho\|_{X}=\sup\limits_{t\in[(0,T]}\|\rho(t)\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in(0,T]}\|t^{\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\rho(t)\|_{C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
For
\widetilde{\rho}\in X
we say that
S(\widetilde{\rho})=\rho
if
\rho(0)=\rho_{0}
and for every
\xi\in C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
t\in(0,T]
we have

(5.25)

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{0},K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg\langle\widetilde{\rho}(s),V_{1}(s,\cdot)(D_{y}K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi)(\cdot)\bigg\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)\,V_{2}(s,dy)\xi(x)\,dx\,ds.

We will show that
S
maps
X
to
X
. Using the triangle inequality we get

\displaystyle|\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\rho_{0},K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\rangle|+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\bigg\langle\widetilde{\rho}(s),V_{1}(s,\cdot)D_{y}K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi(\cdot)\bigg\rangle\bigg|\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)||\xi(x)|\,|V_{2}(s,dy)|\,dx\,ds=:I_{1}(t)+I_{2}(t)+I_{3}(t).

We estimate
I_{1}
as follows:

\displaystyle I_{1}(t)\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Let
\delta>0
be small and assume that
\gamma\in(1,\underline{\alpha})
(for
\gamma=1
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
the argument for
I_{1}
needs to be modified in a rather apparent way). By the interpolation inequality for Hölder norms (see, e.g.,
[
57
, Corollary 1.7]
and
[
27
, Corollary 6.2]
) we get

(5.26)

\displaystyle\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n+1-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2-\gamma-\delta}\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n+2-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\gamma+\delta-1}.

We estimate the terms using
(K)
:

\displaystyle\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n+1-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|K_{t}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D_{y}K_{t}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\|K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n+2-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(\|K_{t}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D_{y}K_{t}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D^{2}_{y}K_{t}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\leq Ct^{-\frac{2}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Using these bounds in (
5.26
) we find that

\displaystyle I_{1}(t)\leq C\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-(2-\gamma-\delta+2\gamma+2\delta-2)/\underline{\alpha}}=C\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-\frac{\gamma+\delta}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

Since
\delta
is arbitrarily small we get that there exists
C_{1}\geq 1
independent of
\rho_{0},V_{1},V_{2},T
, such that

(5.27)

\displaystyle I_{1}(t)\leq C_{1}\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

We now estimate the second term using the definition of
X
:

\displaystyle I_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\bigg|\bigg\langle\widetilde{\rho}(s),V_{1}(s,\cdot)D_{y}K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi(\cdot)\bigg\rangle\bigg|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\|\widetilde{\rho}(s)\|_{C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|V_{1}\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|D_{y}K^{\ast}_{t-s}\ast\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,ds

\displaystyle\hskip-11.0pt\mathop{\leq}^{\ref{eq:K},\widetilde{\rho}\in X}2c(V_{1})C_{1}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV})\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{0}^{t}s^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds

\displaystyle=2c(V_{1})C_{1}(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV})\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{1-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\int_{0}^{1}v^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}(1-v)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,dv

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}(V_{1})(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV})T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

The last term is straightforward, we use
(K)
to get

\displaystyle I_{3}(t)\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV}\|\xi\|_{\infty}\mathcal{K}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,ds\leq C_{3}T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV}\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By summing up the estimates we get

\displaystyle|\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq(C_{1}+C_{2}(V_{1})T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}+C_{3}T^{1-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}+\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}})(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV})\|\xi\|_{C^{n-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

Therefore, if we take
T
sufficiently small, we get
\sup\limits_{t\in(0,T]}\|t^{\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\rho(t)\|_{C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq M
. A similar (but easier) argument shows that
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho(t)\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
. In order to get that
\rho
is continuous in time in respective norms we first make the following observation: if we repeat the above estimates with
\xi\in C^{n-\underline{\alpha}+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for
\delta>0
small enough (in
I_{2}
we have to use interpolation), then we get that for every
0<\varepsilon<T
we have
\rho(\varepsilon)\in C^{-n+\underline{\alpha}-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By using Lemma
B.3
on
(\varepsilon,t)
(see also (
B.4
)) we therefore get that
\rho\in C((\varepsilon,T],C^{-n+\underline{\alpha}-2\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Since
\delta
and
\varepsilon
can be arbitrarily small, we get that
\rho\in C((0,T],C^{-n+\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

The last thing to verify is that
\rho(t)
defined in (
5.25
) is measure representable, i.e.,
\rho(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t\in(0,T]
. In the terms with
\rho_{0}
and
V_{2}
a simple use of Fubini’s theorem suffices (we use the fact that
\rho_{0}
is measure representable). For the term with
V_{1}
we note that by the definition of
X
and the fact that
V_{1}\in(C_{b}((0,T),C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
we have
\Xi:=K_{t-\cdot}\ast\Div(V_{1}(\cdot)\widetilde{\rho}(\cdot))\in C_{b}((0,T),\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(note that
\langle\Xi(s),\xi\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\rho}(s),V_{1}(s)D_{y}K^{\ast}_{t-s}\ast\xi\rangle
). Therefore,
\Xi\colon(0,T)\to\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is measurable and separably valued (any continuous function on an interval is separably valued), hence strongly measurable, see
[
45
, Corollary 1.1.10]
. By
[
45
, Proposition 1.2.3]
and the completeness of
\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we find that
\int_{0}^{t}\Xi(s)\,ds\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and by
[
45
, (1.2)]
, for every
\xi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\langle\Xi(s),\xi\rangle\,ds=\langle\int_{0}^{t}\Xi(s)\,ds,\xi\rangle

This proves that
\rho(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, therefore we can conclude that
S
maps
X
to itself.

Thanks to linearity, verification that
S
is a contraction for small
T
is very similar. From Banach’s fixed point theorem we infer that
S
has a fixed point in
X
. By a Grönwall inequality argument similar to the one in the proof of (
3.2
), we get

(5.28)

\displaystyle\|\rho(T)\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(V_{1},T)(\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|V_{2}(t)\|_{TV}),

with
C(V_{1},T)
locally bounded in
T\in[0,\infty)
.
Therefore the solution can be extended in time indefinitely. We call the so-obtained
\rho
a mild solution and by Lemma
C.2
we get that it is a very weak solution as well.

Note that by the above arguments we get (
5.24
) for small times, say on
[0,T_{1}]
, with
T_{1}
independent of
\rho_{0}
. In order to get the estimates for larger times we note that
\|t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\rho(t)\|_{C^{\gamma-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\approx C(T)\|\rho(t)\|_{C^{\gamma-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for
t\in[T_{1}/2,T]
. Therefore the estimate for any
t\geq T_{1}
can be obtained by using short time estimates on the interval
[t-T_{1}/2,t+T_{1}/2]
and (
5.28
).
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
(z,\rho)
be the solution of the linear system (
5.7
) with
b,c,z_{T}=0
and
\rho_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
k\geq 1
. Then for every
\gamma\in(0,\underline{\alpha})
and
t\in(t_{0},T)
,
\rho(t)
can be extended to a functional
\widetilde{\rho}(t)\in C^{\gamma-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the way that
\widetilde{\rho}\in C((t_{0},T],C^{\gamma-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and

\displaystyle\|t^{\frac{\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\widetilde{\rho}(t)\|_{C^{\gamma-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C(V)\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\rho}\|_{L^{1}(C^{\gamma-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C(V)\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Furthermore, for every
t\in[t_{0},T]
we have
\widetilde{\rho}(t)|_{C^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

We use Lemma
5.7
with
V_{1}=V
and
V_{2}=m\Gamma Dz
and
n=k+1
(note that the assumptions are satisfied). Let
\widetilde{\rho}
be the solution obtained in Lemma
5.7
and let
w
solve (
5.18
). Then
w
is an admissible test function for
\rho-\widetilde{\rho}
in the sense of Lemma
C.2
and we find that
\langle(\rho-\widetilde{\rho})(\tau),\xi\rangle=0
. Since
\xi\in C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is arbitrary, we find that
\rho(\tau)
and
\widetilde{\rho}(\tau)
are identical as elements of
C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which means that
\widetilde{\rho}
is an extension of
\rho
. Note that by Theorem
5.5
we have
\|m\Gamma Dz\|_{TV}\leq C\|Dz\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, therefore the estimates indeed depend only on
\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
∎

5.3.
Existence and properties of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}

As announced earlier, we will obtain
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
as a solution to a certain linear system of the form (
5.7
). We first define a candidate for
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
in Proposition
5.10
and prove its regularity. Then, in Proposition
5.11
we show that the candidate function from Proposition
5.10
indeed satisfies the definition of
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
.

We start with an elementary technical lemma, which we use to reduce the assumptions on
H
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1-\sigma)
and assume that
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\|f(\cdot,y_{1}(\cdot))-f(\cdot,y_{2}(\cdot))\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2\|f\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(2+\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\sigma})\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Let
\phi(x)=f(x,y_{1}(x))
and
h(x)=(0,y_{2}(x)-y_{1}(x))
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|f(\cdot,y_{1}(\cdot))-f(\cdot,y_{2}(\cdot))\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

By an elementary calculation we get
\|\varphi\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 2\|\varphi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}
. Therefore,

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{\infty}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}.

We will estimate the terms separately. We have

\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(1+\|h\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\sigma+\varepsilon})+\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\|h\|_{\infty}^{\sigma+\varepsilon}\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Going back to (
5.29
) we obtain

\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot+h(\cdot))-\phi(\cdot)\|_{C^{\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\big(\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(2+\|h\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\sigma+\varepsilon})\big)^{\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}(\|h\|_{\infty}^{\sigma+\varepsilon}\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\phi\|_{C^{\sigma+\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(2+\|h\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\sigma})\|h\|_{\infty}^{\varepsilon},

which ends the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
, assume that
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
,
(K)
,
(F1)
,
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
,
(G1(

σ

))
,
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
,
(M1)
,
(M2)
, and
(M3)
hold,
\rho_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and let
(u,m)
be the unique solution of the MFG system (
1.1
) with starting time
t_{0}
and initial measure
m_{0}
, obtained in Theorem
4.3
. Then, the system

(5.30)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}z-\mathcal{L}z+D_{p}H(x,Du)\cdot Dz=\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m(t)),\rho(t)\rangle\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\partial_{t}\rho-\mathcal{L}^{*}\rho-\Div(\rho D_{p}H(x,Du))-\Div(mD^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du)Dz)=0\quad&{\rm in}\ (t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
z(T,x)=\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m(T)),\rho(T)\rangle,\quad\rho(t_{0})=\rho_{0}.\end{cases}

has a unique classical solution
(z,\rho)
, such that
z\in C_{b}([t_{0},T),C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\rho\in C_{b}((t_{0},T],C^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore, if we let
(z_{y},\rho_{y})
be the solutions of (
5.30
) with
\rho_{0}=\delta_{y}
and define
J\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(5.31)

\displaystyle J(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)=z_{y}(t_{0},x),

then
J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},\cdot)\in C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, the derivatives up to the order
D^{3,2}_{x,y}
are uniformly continuous with respect to
t_{0}
and
m_{0}
, and there exists
C
independent of
(t_{0},m_{0})
such that

(5.32)

\displaystyle\|J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C.

Moreover, for any finite signed Borel measure
\mu
, we have
\langle J(t_{0},x,m_{0},\cdot),\mu\rangle=z(t_{0},x)
, where
(z,\rho)
is the solution of (
5.30
) with
\rho_{0}=\mu
.

Proof.

1) (
Existence of the derivatives
) Existence and uniqueness of
J
and its regularity in
x
follows from Theorem
5.5
and the regularity for the MFG system given in Theorem
4.3
. Let
e_{i}
be a unit coordinate vector and let
h\neq 0
. Since the system (
5.30
) is linear, we get

\displaystyle\frac{J(t_{0},x,m_{0},y+he_{i})-J(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)}{h}=w_{h}(t_{0},x),

where
w_{h}
is the solution to the first equation of (
5.30
) corresponding to the initial condition
\frac{1}{h}(\delta_{y+he_{i}}-\delta_{y})
. Furthermore,
\frac{1}{h}(\delta_{y+he_{i}}-\delta_{y})
converges to
\partial_{i}\delta_{y}
in
C^{-2-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore if we let
w
be the solution corresponding to
\rho_{0}=\partial_{i}\delta_{y}
, by using estimate (
5.9
) (note that the constant
M
depends only on
\rho_{0}
in our case) we obtain that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},y+he_{i})-J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},y)}{h}-w(t_{0},\cdot)\bigg\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\|w_{h}(t_{0},\cdot)-w(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\bigg\|\frac{1}{h}(\delta_{y+he_{i}}-\delta_{y})-\partial_{i}\delta_{y}\bigg\|_{C^{-2-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{h\to 0}\ 0.

In particular,
\partial_{y_{i}}J(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)=w(t_{0},x)
. We can do a similar procedure to get the second derivative in
y
for
J
. By considering the system (
5.30
) with
\rho_{0}=\frac{\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}-\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y^{\prime}}}{|y-y^{\prime}|^{\sigma}}\in C^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and using estimates (
5.9
) we find that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{\partial^{2}_{y_{i}y_{j}}J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},y)-\partial^{2}_{y_{i}y_{j}}J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0},y^{\prime})}{|y-y^{\prime}|^{\sigma}}\bigg\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\bigg\|\frac{\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}-\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y^{\prime}}}{|y-y^{\prime}|^{\sigma}}\bigg\|_{C^{-2-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The last expression is bounded with respect to
y
and
y^{\prime}
, because for every
\phi\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle|\langle\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}-\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y^{\prime}},\phi\rangle|\leq|D^{2}\phi(y)-D^{2}\phi(y^{\prime})|\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|y-y^{\prime}|^{\sigma}.

This proves (
5.32
).

2) (
Regularity in
m_{0}
and
t_{0}
) To show the continuity in
m_{0}
, we first consider
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we let
(u^{1},m^{1})
,
(u^{2},m^{2})
be the corresponding solutions to the MFG system (
1.1
) and
(z^{1},\rho^{1})
,
(z^{2},\rho^{2})
the corresponding solutions to the linear system (
5.30
) with
\rho_{0}=\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}
(or
\delta_{y}
,
\partial_{i}\delta_{y}
, for
J
and
\partial_{i}J
respectively). Then
\frac{\partial^{2}_{ij}}{\partial y^{2}}J(t_{0},x,m_{0}^{1},y)-\frac{\partial^{2}_{ij}}{\partial y^{2}}J(t_{0},x,m_{0}^{2},y)=z^{1}(t_{0},y)-z^{2}(t_{0},y)
and the difference
(z,\rho):=(z^{1}-z^{2},\rho^{1}-\rho^{2})
satisfies the linear system (
5.7
) with the following coefficients:

(5.33)

\begin{split}\rho_{0}&=0,\quad V(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,Du^{1}),\quad\Gamma(t,x)=D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{1}),\\
b(t,x)&=(D_{p}H(x,Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{2}))Dz^{2}+\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(t))-\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m^{2}(t)),\rho^{2}(t)\bigg\rangle=b_{1}+b_{2},\\
c&=(D_{p}H(x,Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{2}))\rho^{2}+(m^{1}D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{1})-m^{2}D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{2}))Dz^{2}=:c_{1}+c_{2},\\
z_{T}(x)&=\bigg\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(T))-\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m^{2}(T)),\rho^{2}(T)\bigg\rangle.\end{split}

In order to estimate the terms we repeatedly use the results of Theorems
4.3
,
5.3
, and
5.5
, which are independent of
m_{0}
. By using in addition
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
and
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
we get

\displaystyle\|z_{T}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|b_{2}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|\rho_{2}(t)\|_{C^{-2-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})\leq C_{1}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

By Lemma
5.9
,
17
17

17

Note that a rough estimate using the fundamental theorem of calculus on
D_{p}H
would require
4+\sigma
derivatives from
H
. It would however yield a stronger result with
d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})
on the right-hand side.

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|b_{1}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|D_{p}H(x,Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{2})\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|z^{2}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|Du^{1}-Du^{2}\|_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}\leq C_{2}d_{0}^{\varepsilon}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

For
c_{2}
we write

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|c_{2}(t)\|_{C^{-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z^{2}(t)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big(\|m^{1}(D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{1})-D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{2}))\|_{C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\|(m^{1}-m^{2})D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{2})\|_{C^{-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|z^{2}(t)\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big(\|D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{1})-D^{2}_{pp}H(x,Du^{2})\|_{\infty}+d_{0}(m^{1}(t),m^{2}(t))\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

In order to estimate
c_{1}
, we use Corollary
5.8

with
\gamma=1+\sigma
and
k=1
:

\displaystyle\|c_{1}(t)\|_{C^{\sigma-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho^{2}(t)\|_{C^{\sigma-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|(D_{p}H(x,Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{2}))\|_{C^{1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1+\sigma}{\underline{\alpha}}}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|u^{1}-u^{2}\|_{C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\widetilde{C}t^{-\frac{1+\sigma}{\underline{\alpha}}}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

It follows that

\displaystyle\|c_{1}\|_{L^{1}(C^{\sigma-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C_{4}d_{0}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

Furthermore, by Corollary
5.8
we have
\rho^{2}(t)\in\mathfrak{C}^{-2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
t\in[t_{0},T]
, and by Theorem
5.5
,
\rho^{2}\in C([t_{0},T],C^{-3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Note that constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}
above are independent of
m_{0}
.
Therefore, we are in a position to use Theorem
5.5
, which gives

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{[t_{0},T]}\|z(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Cd_{0}^{\varepsilon}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

with
C
independent of
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}
. This proves the uniform continuity with respect to
m_{0}
of derivatives up to order
D^{3,2}_{x,y}
of
J
.

For continuity in
t_{0}
we let
0\leq t_{0}^{1}<t_{0}^{2}\leq T
and we consider
(u^{1},m^{1})
and
(u^{2},m^{2})
which solve the MFG system (
1.1
) on the interval
[t_{0}^{1},T]
,
[t_{0}^{2},T]
respectively, both with initial measure
m_{0}
. We let
(z^{1},\rho^{1})
,
(z^{2},\rho^{2})
the corresponding solutions to the linear system (
5.30
) with
\rho_{0}=\delta_{y}
(or
\partial_{i}\delta_{y}
,
\partial^{2}_{ij}\delta_{y}
to handle the derivatives in
y
). Then

\displaystyle J(t_{0}^{1},x,m_{0},y)-J(t_{0}^{2},x,m_{0},y)=z^{1}(t_{0}^{1},x)-z^{2}(t_{0}^{2},x)=z^{1}(t_{0}^{1},x)-z^{1}(t_{0}^{2},x)+z^{1}(t_{0}^{2},x)-z^{2}(t_{0}^{2},x).

The term
z^{1}(t_{0}^{1},x)-z^{1}(t_{0}^{2},x)
converges to 0 (along with
x
derivatives up to order
3
) uniformly in
x,y,m_{0}
because of the regularity of the solutions to (
5.30
). For the remaining term we first note that
(z^{1},\rho^{1})
satisfies the system (
5.30
) on
[t_{0}^{2},T]
with
\rho_{0}=\rho^{1}(t_{0}^{2})
. Therefore
(z,\rho):=(z^{1}-z^{2},\rho^{1}-\rho^{2})
satisfies (
5.7
) on
[t_{0}^{2},T]
with coefficients same as in (
5.33
) excluding
\rho_{0}
, which here is equal to
\rho^{1}(t_{0}^{2})-\delta_{y}
.
The rest of the arguments is similar as in the case of continuity in
m_{0}
, but here we also use the
t_{0}
stability of the MFG system given in Lemma
D.1
.

3) (
Pairing with measures
) Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We let
z(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}z_{y}(t,x)\,\mu(dy)
and
\rho(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{y}(t)\,\mu(dy)
, with the latter integral understood in the distributional sense, i.e.,
\langle\rho(t),\phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\rho_{y}(t),\phi\rangle\,\mu(dy)
for
\phi\in C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By uniform bounds for derivatives of
z_{y}
and the fact that
\mu
is a finite measure we can use the dominated convergence theorem to see that
z
satisfies the first equation in (
5.30
) – note that we get
\langle\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m(t)),\rho(t)\rangle
on the right-hand side immediately from the definition of
\rho
. The derivatives of
\rho_{y}
in the second equation also become derivatives of
\rho
after the integration. Furthermore, by uniform bounds on
Dz_{y}
and Fubini’s theorem we find that for every
\phi\in C([t_{0},T],C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{pp}^{2}H(x,Du)Dz_{y}(s,x)D\phi(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\,\mu(dy)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{pp}^{2}H(x,Du)Dz(s,x)D\phi(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds.

Therefore
\rho
satisfies the second equation. It is also easy to see that the integrated terminal conditions for
z_{y}
become
z(T,x)=\langle\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m(T)),\rho(T)\rangle
and
\rho(t_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta_{y}\,\mu(dy)=\mu
in the distributional sense.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
U
be the function defined in Definition
5.1
and let
J
be the function obtained in Proposition
5.10
. Then there exist
C,\varepsilon>0
such that for all
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\|U(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0}^{2})-U(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0}^{1})-\langle J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0}^{1},\cdot),m_{0}^{2}-m_{0}^{1}\rangle_{y}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq Cd_{0}^{1+\varepsilon}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}).

In particular,
U
is
C^{1}
in the sense of Definition
2.17
and

\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)=J(t_{0},x,m_{0},y).

Remark 5.12
.

As before, by having only
d_{0}^{1+\varepsilon}
instead of
d_{0}^{2}
we may weaken the assumption on
H
to have only 4 derivatives instead of
4+\sigma
, see the term
b_{2}
in the proof.

Proof.

Let
(u^{1},m^{1})
and
(u^{2},m^{2})
be the solutions of the MFG system with initial measures
m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}
respectively. We have
U(t_{0},x,m_{0}^{1})=u^{1}(t_{0},x)
and
U(t_{0},x,m_{0}^{2})=u^{2}(t_{0},x)
. Note that by the last part of Proposition
5.10
we have that
\langle J(t_{0},\cdot,m_{0}^{1},\cdot),m_{0}^{2}-m_{0}^{1}\rangle_{y}=w(t,y)
, where
(w,\mu)
solves the system (
5.30
) with
(u,m)=(u^{1},m^{1})
, under initial condition
\mu(t_{0})=m_{0}^{2}-m_{0}^{1}
.

By subtracting the respective equations and using the fundamental theorem of calculus we find that the pair
(z,\rho):=(u^{2}-u^{1}-w,m^{2}-m^{1}-\mu)
solves the system (
5.7
) with coefficients

\displaystyle V(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,Du),\quad\Gamma(t,x)=D_{pp}^{2}H(x,Du),\quad\rho_{0}=0,

\displaystyle c=(m^{2}-m^{1})D_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,Du)(Du^{2}-Du^{1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+m^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\big(D_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,\lambda Du^{2}+(1-\lambda)Du^{1})-D_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,Du^{1})\big)(Du^{2}-Du^{1})\,d\lambda=:c_{1}+c_{2},

\displaystyle b(t,x)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m^{2}(t)+(1-\lambda)m^{1}(t),y)-\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(t),y)\bigg)(m^{2}(t,dy)-m^{1}(t,dy))\,d\lambda

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\int_{0}^{1}(D_{p}H(x,\lambda Du^{2}+(1-\lambda)Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{1}))(Du^{2}-Du^{1})\,d\lambda=:b_{1}(t,x)+b_{2}(t,x),

\displaystyle z_{T}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m^{2}(T)+(1-\lambda)m^{1}(T),y)-\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(T),y)\bigg)(m^{2}(T,dy)-m^{1}(T,dy))\,d\lambda.

Therefore, by Theorem
5.5
it suffices to show that the appropriate norms of
b,c,z_{T}
are bounded by
Cd_{0}^{1+\varepsilon}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2})
. By Theorems
4.3
and
5.3
,
(H1)
,
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
, and
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
we find that

\displaystyle\|c_{1}(t)\|_{C^{-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|c_{1}(t)\|_{C^{-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq c\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\big(d_{0}(m^{1}(t),m^{2}(t))\cdot\|Du^{2}(t)-Du^{1}(t)\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)\leq Cd_{0}^{2}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|c_{2}(t)\|_{C^{-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|c_{2}(t)\|_{C^{-0}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\bigg\|\int_{0}^{1}\big(D_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,\lambda Du^{2}+(1-\lambda)Du^{1})-D_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,Du^{1})\big)(Du^{2}-Du^{1})\,d\lambda\bigg\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\lambda D_{ppp}^{3}H(\cdot,s(\lambda Du^{2}+(1-\lambda)Du^{1})+(1-s)Du^{1})|Du^{2}-Du^{1}|^{2}\,d\lambda\,ds\bigg\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq c\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\|Du^{2}(t)-Du^{1}(t)\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq Cd_{0}^{2}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|b_{1}(t)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[t_{0},T]\\
\lambda\in[0,1]\end{subarray}}\bigg\|\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m^{2}(t)+(1-\lambda)m^{1}(t),y)-\frac{\delta{F}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(t),y)\bigg\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}d_{0}(m^{1}(t),m^{2}(t))

\displaystyle\leq cd_{0}^{2}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|b_{2}(t)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|Du^{2}(t,\cdot)-Du^{1}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sup\limits_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\|(D_{p}H(x,\lambda Du^{2}+(1-\lambda)Du^{1})-D_{p}H(x,Du^{1}))\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}c\|Du^{2}(t,\cdot)-Du^{1}(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{2+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{1+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{0}^{1+\varepsilon}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|z_{T}\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\bigg\|\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,\lambda m^{2}(T)+(1-\lambda)m^{1}(T),y)-\frac{\delta{G}}{\delta m}(x,m^{1}(T),y)\bigg\|_{C^{3+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}d_{0}(m^{1}(T),m^{2}(T))

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{0}^{2}(m_{0}^{1},m_{0}^{2}),

where in
(\ast)
we used Lemma
5.9
. We finish the proof by applying Theorem
5.5
.
∎

6.
The master equation

We finally prove well-posedness for the master equation. This is the main result of our paper. It extends previous results to nonlocal (and mixed local-nonlocal) diffusions and
from compact domains to the whole space. We use the scheme of
[
13
]
: having obtained all the regularity needed for
\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
, we compute the time derivative of
U
to get existence of solutions. Then we reconstruct the solutions of the MFG system from the master equation in order to get uniqueness.

Definition 6.1
.

We say that
V\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is a classical solution of the master equation (
1.3
), if
\partial_{t}V,D_{x}V
,
D^{2}_{x}V
exist, and are bounded and uniformly continuous in all variables,

\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,\cdot,m,\cdot)\in C^{2,2}_{b,x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
with all the derivatives bounded and uniformly continuous in all variables,
and (
1.3
) is satisfied pointwise.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
and assume that
(H1)
,
(H2)
,
(H3)
,
(K)
,
(F1)
,
(F2(1,
\sigma
))
,
(G1(

σ

))
,
(G2(1,
\sigma
))
, and
(M1)
,
(M2)
,
(M3)
hold.
Then
U
defined in (
5.1
) is the unique classical solution of the master equation (
1.3
).

Remark 6.3
.

Most of the work in this paper, including new ideas and techniques, was done in the previous sections where we established sufficient regularity of the function
U
. This involved proving new or refined results for mild solutions, for individual equations and linearized systems, in a range of function spaces including negative Hölder spaces. Our assumptions on the nonlocal operator
\mathcal{L}
are quite optimal for the subcritical regime.
This setting is made possible by applying
the arguments of
[
23
]
to get a theory
without moment assumptions
– the first in the context of master equations.
Considerable effort and original techniques have been put into making the many approximation and compactness arguments rigorous.
This includes introducing and working with measure representable functionals and the use of
L^{1}
compactness theory for negative Hölder spaces. The classical solution setting for the master equation is known to impose excess regularity assumptions on the data. We were able to reduce these by obtaining more optimal estimates through repeated use of interpolation
as well as by adapting weighted in time regularity results of
[
64
]
to our setting.

Proof.

1) We start with the existence part. Fix
t_{0}>0
,
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
h>0
.
18
18

18

We only compute the right derivative and prove that it is continuous. Then, by, e.g.,
[
62
, Corollary 2.1.2]

U
is continuously differentiable.
Then

(6.1)

\displaystyle\frac{U(t_{0}+h,x,m_{0})-U(t_{0},x,m_{0})}{h}=

\displaystyle\frac{U(t_{0}+h,x,m(t_{0}+h))-U(t_{0},x,m_{0})}{h}

(6.2)

\displaystyle-\frac{U(t_{0}+h,x,m(t_{0}+h))-U(t_{0}+h,x,m_{0})}{h}.

Obviously the left-hand side of (
6.1
) converges to
\partial_{t}^{+}U(t_{0},x,m_{0})
as
h\to 0^{+}
, provided that the limit exists. We will show that both (
6.2
) and the right-hand side of (
6.1
) converge and sum up to the right-hand side of the master equation. By the uniqueness for the MFG system (
1.1
) and the definition of
U
we have
U(t_{0}+h,x,m(t_{0}+h))=u(t_{0}+h,x)
. By this and the equation for
u
in (
1.1
),

\displaystyle\frac{U(t_{0}+h,x,m(t_{0}+h))-U(t_{0},x,m_{0})}{h}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{h\to 0^{+}}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}_{x}u(t_{0},x)+H(x,D_{x}u(t_{0},x))-F(x,m_{0})

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{L}_{x}U(t_{0},x,m_{0})+H(x,D_{x}U(t_{0},x,m_{0}))-F(x,m_{0}).

In order to handle (
6.2
) we use the fundamental theorem of calculus (
2.8
):

\displaystyle\frac{U(t_{0}+h,x,m(t_{0}+h))-U(t_{0}+h,x,m_{0})}{h}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)\,(m(t_{0}+h)-m_{0})(dy)\,d\lambda.

By Propositions
5.10
and

5.11
the function
\phi(t,y)=\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,sm(t_{0}+h)+(1-s)m_{0},y)
(note that
\phi
is constant in
t
) is twice differentiable in
y
with the derivatives bounded. Therefore it can be passed as a test function (see (
3.21
)) in the equation for
m
in (
1.1
), yielding

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)\,(m(t_{0}+h)-m_{0})(dy)\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)

(6.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt-D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)\cdot D_{p}H(y,Du(t,y))\bigg)\,m(t,dy)\,dt\,d\lambda.

We will show that, as
h\to 0^{+}
, the last expression converges to

(6.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\ -D_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\cdot D_{p}H(y,Du(t_{0},y))\bigg)\,m(t_{0},dy).

We discuss the limiting procedure only for the term
\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
, the other works similarly. We first split:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)\,m(t,dy)\,dt\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0}+h,x,\lambda m(t_{0}+h)+(1-\lambda)m_{0},y)-\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\bigg)\,m(t,dy)\,dt\,d\lambda

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\,m(t,dy)\,dt=I_{1}(h)+I_{2}(h).

By Propositions
5.10
and
5.11
and the fact that
m\in C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we get that
\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}
is uniformly continuous in
t
and
m
, hence the integrand in
I_{1}
converges to 0 uniformly in
y
. Therefore
I_{1}(h)\to 0
as
h\to 0^{+}
.

In
I_{2}
we do another splitting:

\displaystyle I_{2}(h)=\frac{1}{h}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)(m(t,dy)-m_{0}(dy))\,dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\,m_{0}(dy).

By Theorem
4.3
and Remark
2.11
we get that
m(t)
converges to
m_{0}
weakly as
t\to t_{0}^{+}
. Therefore, since
\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we get that there is a modulus of continuity
\omega
such that

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{h}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)(m(t,dy)-m_{0}(dy))\,dt\bigg|\leq\frac{1}{h}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\omega(h)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{h\to 0^{+}}0.

It follows that

\displaystyle I_{2}(h)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{h\to 0^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{U}}{\delta m}(t_{0},x,m_{0},y)\,m_{0}(dy).

By the above arguments and a similar procedure for the second term in (
6.3
) we find that (
6.3
) converges to (
6.4
), which shows that
U
satisfies the master equation.

2) Now we proceed to the uniqueness. Let
V
be a classical solution to the master equation. We will show that
V=U
. Fix
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and consider the following Fokker–Planck equation:

(6.5)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\widetilde{m}-\mathcal{L}^{*}\widetilde{m}-\Div(\widetilde{m}D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,\widetilde{m})))=0,\quad{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\widetilde{m}(t_{0})=m_{0}.\end{cases}

We will show that this equation has a distributional solution in class
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by using the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem. Let
\psi
be a tightness measuring function for
m_{0}
and
\nu\textbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}
from Lemma
2.13
and define

X=\bigg\{m\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):m(0)=m_{0},\,\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m(t,dx)\leq C_{1},\,\sup\limits_{t_{0}\leq s,t\leq T}\frac{d_{0}(m(t),m(s))}{|t-s|^{1/2}}\leq C_{2}\bigg\},

where the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
are independent of
m
and will be specified later. We define the map
T
on
X
as follows:
T(m)=\mu
if
\mu\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a distributional solution of the problem

(6.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mu-\mathcal{L}^{*}\mu-\Div(\mu D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,m)))=0\quad{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\mu(t_{0})=m_{0}.\end{cases}

1.

T
is well-defined.
This follows from Theorem
3.9
with

(6.7)

\displaystyle b(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,m(t))).

We have
b\in(C([t_{0},T],C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
because of
(H1)
, the definition of the classical solution thanks to which
V,D_{x}V,

D^{2}_{xx}V
are bounded and uniformly continuous in all variables, and the definition of
X
which gives the continuity of
m
with respect to
t
.

2.

T\colon X\to X
. We already have
\mu\in C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\mu(t_{0})=m_{0}
. Tightness and Hölder continuity also follow from Theorem
3.9
. Indeed, it states that

(6.8)

\displaystyle d_{0}(\mu(t),\mu(s))\leq c_{0}(1+\|b\|_{\infty})|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad{\rm and}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,\mu(t,dx)

(6.9)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m_{0}(dx)+\|D\psi\|_{\infty}\bigg(2+cT\big(\|A\|_{2}+\|B\|_{\infty}+\|b\|_{\infty}+\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\,\nu(dz)\big)\bigg)+T\int_{|z|\geq 1}\psi(z)\,\nu(dz),

for some constants
c_{0},c
independent of
m
. Furthermore,
\|b\|_{\infty}\leq C
, for some
C>0
independent of
m
, therefore by setting

C_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\,m_{0}(dx)+\|D\psi\|_{\infty}\bigg(2+cT\big(\|A\|_{2}+\|B\|_{\infty}+C+\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\,\nu(dz)\big)\bigg)+T\int_{|z|\geq 1}\psi(z)\,\nu(dz),

C_{2}=c_{0}(1+C),

we get that
\mu\in X
.

3.

X
is convex and compact in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Convexity is straightforward. For compactness we use a metric version of the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, see Kelley
[
50
, Chapter 7 §17]
with
C=C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
F=X
. By the Hölder condition (
6.8
) we get equicontinuity, whereas by tightness ((
6.9
) and Lemma
2.13
) we obtain pointwise relative compactness. Therefore, by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem
X
is relatively compact in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. To see that
X
is compact, it suffices to show that it is closed, see Remark
2.11
(b). To this end, take a sequence
(m_{n})\subset X
converging to
m
in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. The Hölder bound is preserved, because of the uniform bound for
m_{n}
. The tightness estimate (
6.9
) is obtained from the fact that for every
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
r>0
,

\int_{B_{r}}\psi(x)\,m(t,dx)=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\int_{B_{r}}\psi(x)\,m_{n}(t,dx)\leq C_{1}.

Thus
m\in X
, which proves that
X
is compact.

4.

T
is continuous.
Assume that
(m_{n})\subset X
converges to
m
in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Since
X
is closed,
m\in X
. Let
\mu_{n}
and
\mu
be the corresponding solutions of (
6.6
). Continuity of
T
means that
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in
C([0,T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. In order to show this convergence we consider
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}=\mu-\mu_{n}
. We have (in the distributional sense)

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\mathcal{L}^{*}\widetilde{\mu}_{n}-\Div(\widetilde{\mu}_{n}D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,m(t))))=\Div(\mu_{n}F_{n}(t,x)),\\
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(t_{0})=0,\end{cases}

where

F_{n}(t,x)=D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,m_{n}(t)))-D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,m(t))).

By
(H1)
and Lemma
2.20
,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\|F_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{H}K_{V}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m(t),m_{n}(t))\qquad\text{where}\qquad K_{V}=\sup_{t,x,m}\Big\|D_{x}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,x,m,\cdot)\Big\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

and
K_{U}\leq\|D_{x}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}\|_{\infty}+\|D_{y}D_{x}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}\|_{\infty}<\infty
by Lemma
2.21
.
The weak formulation of (
6.10
) reads

(6.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t,x)\,\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(t,dx)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi-b\cdot D\phi)\,\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(s,dx)\,ds+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi(s,x)F_{n}(s,x)\,\mu_{n}(s,dx)\,ds,

where
b
is the same as in (
6.7
) (in particular,
b\in(C([t_{0},T],C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
) and
\phi\in C^{1,2}_{b}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is arbitrary. Let
\phi_{0}\in C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By Lemma
3.3
there exists
\phi
such that the integrand in the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.12
) vanishes,
\phi(t)=\phi_{0}
, and
\|D\phi\|_{\infty}\leq\widetilde{C}
with
\widetilde{C}
independent of
\phi_{0}
, because
\|\phi_{0}\|_{C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1
. By this and (
6.11
) we get

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{0}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(t,dx)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D\phi(s,x)F_{n}(s,x)|\,\mu_{n}(s,dx)\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\|D\phi\|_{\infty}\|F_{n}\|_{\infty}(t-t_{0})\leq C(T-t_{0})\|D\phi\|_{\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n}(t),m(t)).

By Lemma
2.16
it follows that

\displaystyle d_{0}(\mu_{n}(t),\mu(t))=\sup\limits_{\phi\in{\rm Lip}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{0}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(t,dx)\bigg|\leq C(T-t_{0})\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{0}(m_{n}(t),m(t)),

therefore
\mu_{n}
converges to
\mu
in
C([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, which proves the continuity of
T
.

By the Schauder–Tychonoff theorem we conclude that the Fokker–Planck equation (
6.5
) has a solution
\widetilde{m}
.

For
t\in[t_{0},T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
v(t,x)=V(t,x,\widetilde{m}(t))
. For
t\in(t_{0},T)
and
h
small we have

(6.13)

\displaystyle\frac{v(t+h,x)-v(t,x)}{h}=\frac{V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t+h))-V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t))}{h}+\frac{V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t))-V(t,x,\widetilde{m}(t))}{h}.

The second term converges to
\partial_{t}V(t,x,\widetilde{m}(t))
as
h\to 0
. For the first term we use the fundamental theorem of calculus from Lemma
2.19
and the distributional formulation of the Fokker–Planck equation for
\widetilde{m}
:

\displaystyle\frac{V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t+h))-V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t))}{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t+h,x,\lambda\widetilde{m}(t+h)+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{m}(t),y)(\widetilde{m}(t+h)-\widetilde{m}(t))(dy)\,d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t}^{t+h}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t+h,x,\lambda\widetilde{m}(t+h)+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{m}(t),y)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-D_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t+h,x,\lambda\widetilde{m}(t+h)+(1-\lambda)\widetilde{m}(t),y)\cdot D_{p}H(y,D_{x}v(s,y))\bigg)\,m(s,dy)\,dt\,d\lambda.

By the definition of the classical solution,
V
and
v
are regular enough so that we can repeat the argument for
U
in the existence part to get

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t+h))-V(t+h,x,\widetilde{m}(t))}{h}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,x,\widetilde{m},y)-D_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,x,\widetilde{m},y)\cdot D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,y,\widetilde{m})))\bigg)\,\widetilde{m}(t,y)\,dy.

By this, (
6.13
) and the master equation for
V
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v(t,x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}V(t,x,\widetilde{m})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,x,\widetilde{m},y)-D_{y}\frac{\delta{V}}{\delta m}(t,x,\widetilde{m},y)\cdot D_{p}H(x,D_{x}V(t,y,\widetilde{m})))\bigg)\,\widetilde{m}(t,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{L}V(t,x,\widetilde{m})+H(x,D_{x}V(t,x,\widetilde{m}))-F(x,\widetilde{m})

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{L}v(t,x)+H(x,D_{x}v(t,x))-F(x,\widetilde{m}).

Therefore
v
satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi equation of (
1.1
) and so
(v,\widetilde{m})
satisfies the MFG system with the starting time
t_{0}
and initial measure
m_{0}
. By the uniqueness of solutions to (
1.1
) we get that
V(t_{0},x,m_{0})=v(t_{0},x)=U(t_{0},x,m_{0})
for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which ends the proof of uniqueness.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma
2.6

Proof.

Let
C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ast}
and let
K=1+d+\ldots+d^{n}
. For
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
we use the convention

\displaystyle\mu=(\mu_{\displaystyle 0},\,\mu_{\displaystyle(1)},\ldots,\mu_{\displaystyle(d)},\,\mu_{\displaystyle(1,1)},\,\mu_{\displaystyle(1,2)},\ldots,\,\mu_{\displaystyle(d,d)},\,\mu_{\displaystyle(1,1,1)},\ldots,\,\mu_{\underbrace{(1,\ldots,1)}_{n}},\ldots,\,\mu_{\underbrace{(d,\ldots,d)}_{n}}),

so for a multi-index
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq n
,
\mu_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the corresponding coordinate of
\mu
.

1) We first make an auxiliary claim: for every
\rho\in C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
such that
\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}}
and

(A.1)

\displaystyle\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In order to prove the claim, we first note that
C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be embedded isometrically into
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
; below we identify
C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with that embedding. Thus, if
\rho\in C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then by the Hahn–Banach theorem it can be extended to
\rho^{*}\in(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K})^{\ast}
with
\|\rho^{*}\|_{(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K})^{\ast}}=\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. By the Riesz representation theorem for
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
such that
\|\rho^{\ast}\|_{(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K})^{*}}=\|\mu\|_{TV}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}\|\mu_{\alpha}\|_{TV}
and

(A.2)

\displaystyle\langle\rho^{\ast},\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}.

Then (
A.1
) holds and
\|\mu\|_{TV}=\|\rho^{*}\|_{(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K})^{\ast}}=\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, which proves the claim.

2) We now prove the statement of the Lemma. Let
(\rho_{k})\subset\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\rho\in C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and assume that
\|\rho_{k}-\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Since
\rho_{k}\in\mathfrak{C}^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists
\mu^{k}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
such that

(A.3)

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{k},\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu^{k}_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

By 1) there is
\tilde{\mu}^{k}
, such that
\|\tilde{\mu}^{k}\|_{TV}=\|\rho_{k}|_{C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and

(A.4)

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{k},\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\tilde{\mu}^{k}_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu^{k}_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We will show that the above equalities hold for all
\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For
R>1
, let
\tau_{R}
be a smooth cut-off function, i.e.,
0\leq\tau_{R}\leq 1
,
\tau_{R}=1
on
B_{R}
,
\tau_{R}=0
on
B_{R+1}^{c}
, and
\|\tau_{R}\|_{C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq M
, with
M
independent of
R
. Then for every
\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
|\alpha|\leq n
we have
\partial^{\alpha}(\tau_{R}\phi)\to\partial^{\alpha}\phi
pointwise as
R\to\infty
. Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem,

(A.5)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}(\tau_{R}\phi)\,d\mu^{k}_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu^{k}_{\alpha}.

Since
\tau_{R}\phi\in C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, this together with (
A.4
) gives

(A.6)

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{k},\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\tilde{\mu}^{k}_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mu}^{k}\|_{TV}=\|\rho_{k}|_{C^{n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|_{C^{-n}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\rho_{k}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|\tilde{\mu}^{k}\|_{TV},

where the second inequality follows from taking the supremum over a larger set and the last inequality can be checked by hand using (
A.6
). Thus,
\|\rho_{k}\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\tilde{\mu}^{k}\|_{TV}
. Therefore, since
\rho_{k}\to\rho
in
C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we find that
(\tilde{\mu}^{k})
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
with the total variation norm, so it converges to some
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{K}
. Then we have
\|\rho\|_{C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\tilde{\mu}\|_{TV}
, and since norm convergence implies weak
∗
convergence, (
A.6
) yields

\displaystyle\langle\rho,\phi\rangle=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,d\mu_{\alpha},\quad\phi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

which ends the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Continuity in time

In this whole section we assume
(K)
. We note that continuity in time was not verified in the Banach fixed point arguments in
[
34
]
. The results of this section cover this issue as well.

We will often use the Duhamel formula on subintervals
[s,t]\subseteq[0,t]
. Here is the first version of this result.

Lemma B.1
.

Assume that
z_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If we let

(B.1)

\displaystyle z(t,x)=K_{t}\ast z_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-\tau,x-y)g(\tau,y)\,dy\,d\tau,\quad t\in[0,T],\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

then for every
s\in[0,t)
,

(B.2)

\displaystyle z(t,x)=(K_{t-s}\ast z(s))(x)+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-\tau,x-y)g(\tau,y)\,dy\,d\tau,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Note that (
B.2
) corresponds to solving the same equation on a shorter time interval.

Proof.

We can assume that
s\in(0,t)
. We use the Fubini–Tonelli theorem and the Chapman–Kolmogorov equations for
K_{t}
(see, e.g.,
[
5
, Theorem 3.1.5]
):

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)K(s,y-z)\,dy=K(t,x-z),\quad 0<s<t,\ x,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

By (
B.1
),

\displaystyle(K_{t-s}\ast z(s))(x)

\displaystyle=K_{t-s}\ast K_{s}\ast z_{0}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(s-\tau,y-z)g(\tau,z)\,dz\,d\tau\,dy

\displaystyle=K_{t}\ast z_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-\tau,x-z)g(\tau,z)\,dz\,d\tau,

which proves our result.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\sigma\in(0,\underline{\alpha}-1)
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,\sigma)
. Assume that
f\in C([0,T],C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
z_{0}\in C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If we define

\displaystyle z(t,x)=(K_{t}\ast z_{0})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)f(s,y)\,dy\,ds,\quad t\in[0,T],\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

then
z\in B([0,T],C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([0,T],C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore, for
0\leq s<t\leq T
we have

\displaystyle\|z(t,\cdot)-z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma-\varepsilon}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq(\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})\omega(t-s),

where the modulus of continuity
\omega
is independent of
z_{0},z,
and
f
.

Proof.

The fact that
z\in B([0,T],C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is shown in the proof of Lemma
3.3
. Let
0\leq s<t\leq T
. By using Duhamel’s formula on
[s,t]
(see Lemma
B.1
) we get

\displaystyle z(t,x)-z(s,x)=(K_{t-s}\ast z(s))(x)-z(s,x)+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-\tau,x-y)f(\tau,y)\,dy\,d\tau.

Therefore, for
1\leq l\leq k
,

\displaystyle D^{l}z(t,x)-D^{l}z(s,x)

\displaystyle=\bigg[(K_{t-s}\ast D^{l}z(s))(x)-D^{l}z(s,x)\bigg]

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-\tau,x-y)D^{l-1}f(\tau,y)\,dy\,d\tau=:I_{1}^{l}(x)+I_{2}^{l}(x).

We will only estimate the
(\sigma-\varepsilon)
-Hölder seminorms of the
k
-th derivative, the rest being similar but easier. Let
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. We have

(B.3)

\displaystyle|D^{k}z(t,x+h)-D^{k}z(t,x)-D^{k}z(s,x+h)+D^{k}z(s,x)|\leq|I_{1}^{k}(x+h)-I_{1}^{k}(x)|+|I_{2}^{k}(x+h)-I_{2}^{k}(x)|.

We first estimate the term with
I_{1}^{k}
. For
|h|\geq 1
we have
\frac{1}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}\leq 1
, so we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{|h|\geq 1}\frac{|I_{1}^{k}(x+h)-I_{1}^{k}(x)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{|h|\geq 1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,y)|D^{k}z(s,x+h-y)-D^{k}z(s,x-y)-D^{k}z(s,x+h)+D^{k}z(s,x)|\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 4\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,y)(1\wedge|y|^{\sigma})\,dy,

which converges to 0 as
t-s\to 0^{+}
, because
K(t-s)\to\delta_{0}
. Now, let
0<|h|<1
. Then

\displaystyle|I_{1}^{k}(x+h)-I_{1}^{k}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,y)|D^{k}z(s,x+h-y)-D^{k}z(s,x-y)-D^{k}z(s,x+h)+D^{k}z(s,x)|\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 2\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg(\int_{B(0,|h|)}K(t-s,y)|y|^{\sigma}\,dy+|h|^{\sigma}\int_{B(0,|h|)^{c}}K(t-s,y)\,dy\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}\bigg(\int_{B(0,|h|)}K(t-s,y)|y|^{\varepsilon}\,dy+|h|^{\varepsilon}\int_{B(0,|h|)^{c}}K(t-s,y)\,dy\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,y)(|h|^{\varepsilon}\wedge|y|^{\varepsilon})\,dy.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{0<|h|<1}\frac{|I_{1}^{k}(x+h)-I_{1}^{k}(x)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}\leq 2\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,y)(1\wedge|y|^{\varepsilon})\,dy

and the remaining integral converges to 0 as
t-s\to 0^{+}
. Hence we get

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|I_{1}^{k}(x+h)-I_{1}^{k}(x)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}\leq\sup\limits_{s\in[0,T]}\|z(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{k+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\omega(t-s),

where the modulus of continuity
\omega
does not depend on
z_{0},z,
and
f
.

The term with
I_{2}^{k}
in (
B.3
) is more straightforward: by Remark
2.22
we get

\displaystyle\frac{|I_{2}^{k}(x+h)-I_{2}^{k}(x)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|D_{x}K(t-\tau,x+h-y)-D_{x}K(t-\tau,x-y)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{s}^{t}(t-\tau)^{-\frac{1+\sigma-\varepsilon}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,d\tau,

where the last constant does not depend on
h,s,
and
t
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|I_{2}^{k}(x+h)-I_{2}^{k}(x)|}{|h|^{\sigma-\varepsilon}}\leq\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{C^{k-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\omega(t-s),

where the modulus of continuity
\omega
does not depend on
z_{0},z,
and
f
.
This ends the proof.
∎

Below we give results of the similar type for negative Hölder norms.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\gamma,\delta>0
and assume that
V_{1}\in(C([0,T],C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
,
V_{2}\in(L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
,
\rho_{0}\in C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\widetilde{\rho}\in L^{\infty}((0,T),C^{-\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. If we define
\rho
for
\xi\in C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
t\in[0,T]
by the formula

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle=\langle\rho_{0},K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\widetilde{\rho}(s),V_{1}(s,\cdot)(D_{y}K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi)(\cdot)\rangle\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K^{\ast}_{t-s}\ast\xi(y)\,V_{2}(s,dy)\,ds,

then
\rho\in C([0,T],C^{-\gamma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Note that a counterpart of (
B.2
) holds: for every
\xi\in C^{\gamma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

(B.4)

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle=\langle\rho(s),K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi\rangle+\int_{s}^{t}\langle\widetilde{\rho}(\tau),V_{1}(\tau,\cdot)(D_{y}K_{t-\tau}^{\ast}\ast\xi)(\cdot)\rangle\,d\tau+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K^{\ast}_{t-\tau}\ast\xi(y)\,V_{2}(\tau,dy)\,d\tau.

Indeed, the equality follows from testing
\rho(s)
with
K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi
and the fact that by the Chapman–Kolmogorov equations,

\displaystyle(D_{y}K_{s-\tau}^{\ast})\ast K_{t-s}^{\ast}=D_{y}(K_{s-\tau}^{\ast}\ast K_{t-s}^{\ast})=D_{y}K_{t-\tau}^{\ast}.

The remainder of the proof consists in a dual estimate of
\rho(t)-\rho(s)
, which uses the fact that
\|K_{t-s}\ast\xi-\xi\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\omega(t-\tau)\|\xi\|_{C^{\gamma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, obtained in the proof of Lemma
B.2
. We skip the details.
∎

In the next estimate we work with very weak solutions appearing in Theorem
5.5
.

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose that all the assumptions of Theorem
5.5
hold and that the pair
(z_{\lambda},\rho_{\lambda})
solves system (
5.14
). Then
\rho_{\lambda}\in C([t_{0},T],C^{-k-1-\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Let
t_{0}\leq t_{2}<t_{1}\leq T
and
\xi\in C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For
i=1,2
we define
\phi_{i}
to be the solution of the following problem:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\phi_{i}(t,x)+\mathcal{L}\phi_{i}(t,x)-V(t,x)D\phi_{i}(t,x)=0,\quad&(t,x)\in(t_{0},t_{i})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\phi_{i}(t_{i},x)=\xi(x),\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases}

By Lemma
3.3
we have that
\phi_{i}\in B([t_{0},T],C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap C([t_{0},T],C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for arbitrarily small
\delta>0
. By equations for
\rho_{\lambda}
and
\phi_{i}
we get

\displaystyle|\langle\rho_{\lambda}(t_{1})-\rho_{\lambda}(t_{2}),\xi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda|\langle\rho_{0},\phi_{1}(t_{0})-\phi_{2}(t_{0})\rangle|+\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\langle D\phi_{1}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\langle D\phi_{2}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds\bigg|

\displaystyle+\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi_{1}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi_{2}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg|=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{C^{-k-1-\sigma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\phi_{1}(t_{0})-\phi_{2}(t_{0})\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\langle D\phi_{1}(s)-D\phi_{2}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds\bigg|+\bigg|\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}\langle D\phi_{1}(s),c(s)\rangle\,ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|c\|_{L^{1}(C^{-k-\sigma+\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\big(\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},t_{2}]}\|\phi_{1}(t)-\phi_{2}(t)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+(t_{1}-t_{2})\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]}\|\phi_{1}(t)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big),

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(D\phi_{1}-D\phi_{2})\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds-\int_{t_{2}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi_{1}\Gamma Dz_{\lambda}(s,x)\,m(s,dx)\,ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\|Dz_{\lambda}(s)\|_{\infty}\big(T\|D\phi_{1}-D\phi_{2}\|_{\infty}+(t_{1}-t_{2})\|D\phi_{1}\|_{\infty}\big).

By the above estimates, the statement of the lemma is true provided that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},t_{2}]}\|\phi_{1}(t)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\ {\rm and}

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},t_{2}]}\|\phi_{1}(t)-\phi_{2}(t)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\omega(t_{1}-t_{2}),

for some modulus of continuity
\omega
. The first inequality follows from Lemma
3.3
. For the second inequality, note that
\phi=\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}
solves the problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)+\mathcal{L}\phi(t,x)-V(t,x)D\phi(t,x)=0,\quad&(t,x)\in(t_{0},t_{2})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\phi(t_{2},x)=\phi_{1}(t_{2},x)-\xi(x)=\phi_{1}(t_{2},x)-\phi_{1}(t_{1},x),\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases}

By Lemma
3.3
(b) we find that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[t_{0},t_{2}]}\|\phi(t)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\phi_{1}(t_{2},x)-\phi_{1}(t_{1},x)\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma-\delta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|\xi\|_{C^{k+1+\sigma}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\omega(t_{2}-t_{1}),

with
\omega
independent of
\xi
. This ends the proof.
∎

Appendix C
From mild to very weak solutions

Lemma C.1
.

Assume that
V_{1}\in(L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d}
,
V_{2}\in(L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{d}
, and
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
\rho\in B([0,T],L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is product measurable and satisfies

(C.1)

\displaystyle\rho(t,x)=K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\,dy\,ds,

for all
t\in[0,T]
, almost everywhere in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then for every
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\partial_{t}\phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and every
t\in[0,T]
we have

(C.2)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(t,x)\rho(t,x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(0,x)\rho_{0}(x)\,dx\\
=&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{t}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(s,x))\rho(s,x)\,dx\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\phi(s,x)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,x)\,dx\,ds.\end{split}

Proof.

We show the result directly for
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\partial_{t}\phi\in C_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Other test functions can be approximated using the smooth cut-off functions
\chi_{R}
defined at the beginning of Section
2
. Indeed, since
\phi\chi_{R}
,
\partial_{t}(\phi\chi_{R})
,
D_{x}(\phi\chi_{R})
,
D^{2}_{x}(\phi\chi_{R})
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}(\phi\chi_{R})
are uniformly bounded and converge to
\phi
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
D_{x}\phi
,
D^{2}_{x}\phi
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}\phi
19
19

19

Note that for
x
fixed and
R>1+|x|
we have
|\mathcal{L}^{\ast}\big(\phi(\cdot,t)-\phi(\cdot,t)\chi_{R}\big)(x)|\leq C(\|\phi(\cdot,t)-\phi(\cdot,t)\chi_{R}\|_{C_{b}^{2}(B(0,R))}+2\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\nu(B(0,R)^{c}))\lesssim 0+\nu(B(0,R)^{c})\to 0
as
R\to\infty
.

pointwise as
R\to\infty
, and
\rho
and
V_{2}
are bounded in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we are in a position to use the dominated convergence theorem.

In order to prove the statement for
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we multiply (
C.1
) by
\phi(t,x)
, integrate over
x
, and subtract
\int\phi(0)\rho_{0}
, getting

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(t,x)\phi(t,x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(0,x)\rho_{0}(x)\,dx

(C.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(t,x)-\phi(0,x)\rho_{0}(x)\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\phi(t,x)\,dy\,ds\,dx.

The goal is then to show that the right-hand side in the equation above is equal to the right-hand side of (
C.2
). We start by investigating the first integral of (
C.3
). By the fundamental theorem of calculus we find that for
h\in(0,t)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(t,x)-K_{h}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(h,x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{h}^{t}\partial_{s}\big((K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x))\phi(s,x)\big)\,ds\,dx.

Since
K
is a fundamental solution, for every
s\in(h,t)
we have

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\big((K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x))\phi(s,x)\big)=(\partial_{s}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(s,x)+K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)=(\mathcal{L}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(s,x)+K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\partial_{s}\phi(s,x),

so by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem,
(K)
and the fact that
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we may interchange the order of integration and get

(C.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(t,x)-K_{h}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(h,x)\,dx=\int_{h}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\big((K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x))\phi(s,x)\big)\,dx\,ds.

Furthermore, we have

(C.5)

\begin{split}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\big((K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x))\phi(s,x)\big)\,dx\bigg|&\leq\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathcal{L}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(s,x)\,dx\bigg|+\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)\,dx\bigg|\\
&=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(s,x)\,dx\bigg|+\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)\,dx\bigg|\leq C(\phi),\end{split}

In the second equality we used
(K)
,
\phi(s)\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\rho_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
20
20

20

We have
K_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{L}K_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, hence
\mathcal{F}((\mathcal{L}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{L}K_{s})\mathcal{F}\rho_{0}=\Psi\mathcal{F}(K_{s}\ast\rho_{0})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{L}(K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}))
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform and
\Psi
is the Fourier multiplier of
\mathcal{L}
. Thus,
(\mathcal{L}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0}=\mathcal{L}(K_{s}\ast\rho_{0})
. Furthermore, since
K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we get
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathcal{L}K_{s})\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(s,x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\mathcal{L}^{\ast}\phi(s,x)\,dx
, see the discussion in Section
2.4
.
Note that
C(\phi)
is independent of
s
.

We let
h\to 0^{+}
in (
C.4
). By (
C.5
), the dominated convergence theorem, and the strong
L^{1}
-continuity of the semigroup corresponding to
K_{t}

[
5
, Theorem 3.4.2]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(K_{t}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(t,x)-\phi(0,x)\rho_{0}(x)\big)\,dx=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}(K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)\phi(s,x))\,dx\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{s}\ast\rho_{0}(x)(\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(s,x))\,dx\,ds=:I_{1}.

Now we will investigate the second integral of (
C.3
):

(C.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\phi(t,x)\,dy\,ds\,dx.

By using Tonelli’s theorem,
(K)
, and integrating in the order
dx\,dy\,ds
,
we see that it converges absolutely, therefore we may interchange the order of integration and get that (
C.6
) is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\phi(t,x)\,dx\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{x}\phi(t,x)\,dx\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)D_{x}\phi(t,x)\,dx-D_{y}\phi(s,y)\bigg)\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{y}\phi(s,y)\,dy\,ds=:J_{1}+J_{2}.

Since
D_{x}\phi\in C([0,T],C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, by using
(K)
, the strong continuity of the semigroup corresponding to
K^{*}_{t}
(recall that
K_{t}(x-y)=K^{\ast}_{t}(y-x)
), the Fubini–Tonelli theorem, and arguments similar to (
C.4
) and (
C.5
),
21
21

21

Note, in particular, that because of
(K)
and
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, for every
\tau\in(s,t)
we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\tau}D_{x}K(\tau-s)\phi(\tau)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\mathcal{L}K(\tau-s)\phi(\tau)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(\tau-s)\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(\tau)
and the last of these integrals is bounded from above by
C(\tau-s)^{-\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}
, which is integrable with respect to
\tau
.
we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(t-s,x-y)D_{x}\phi(t,x)\,dx-D_{y}\phi(s,y)=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\tau}\big(D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\phi(\tau,x)\big)\,dx\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\tau}D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau+\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\partial_{\tau}\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau.

Therefore,

\displaystyle J_{1}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\partial_{\tau}K(\tau-s,x-y)D_{x}\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\partial_{\tau}\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau\,dy\,ds=:J_{1,1}+J_{1,2}.

We have

\displaystyle J_{1,1}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)\mathcal{L}K(\tau-s,x-y)D_{x}\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(\tau,x)\,dx\,d\tau\,dy\,ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(\tau,x)\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\,dy\,ds\,dx\,d\tau.

The change in the order of integration is allowed because the second integral is absolutely convergent. Furthermore,

\displaystyle J_{1,2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\tau}\phi(\tau,x)\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,y)D_{x}K(\tau-s,x-y)\,dy\,ds\,dx\,d\tau.

Hence, switching the role of
s
and
\tau
we get

\displaystyle J_{1}=J_{1,1}+J_{1,2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(s,x))\int_{0}^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(\tau,y)D_{x}K(s-\tau,x-y)\,dy\,d\tau\,dx\,ds.

We finish the proof by noticing that (
C.1
) yields

\displaystyle(I_{1}+J_{1})+J_{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{s}\phi(s,x)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\phi(s,x))\rho(s,x)\,dx\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{x}\phi(s,x)(V_{1}\rho+V_{2})(s,x)\,dx\,ds.

∎

In the next result we discuss mild solutions in negative order Hölder spaces. The arguments are quite similar, but we present certain parts of the proof in full detail in order to pinpoint the class of admissible test functions. This is crucial for identification of solutions
\rho
obtained in Theorem
5.5
and Lemma
5.7
.

Lemma C.2
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma
5.7
hold true. Let
\rho\in C_{b}((0,T],C^{-n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be the mild solution obtained by fixed point argument in the proof of Lemma
5.7
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho_{0},K_{t}^{\ast}\ast\xi\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg\langle\rho(s),V_{1}(s,\cdot)(D_{y}K_{t-s}^{\ast}\ast\xi)(\cdot)\bigg\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D_{y}K(t-s,x-y)\,V_{2}(s,dy)\xi(x)\,dx\,ds,\quad t\in[0,T],\ \xi\in C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Then
\rho
is a distributional
solution of (
5.23
), that is, for every
\phi\in C([0,T],C^{n}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
D^{n}\phi
is uniformly continuous,
\partial_{t}\phi\in C_{b}((0,T),C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi\in B([0,T],C^{n-1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

we have

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\phi(t)\rangle-\langle\rho_{0},\phi(0)\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle(\partial_{t}\phi+\mathcal{L}\phi-V_{1}D\phi)(s),\rho(s)\rangle\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}D\phi(s,y)\,V_{2}(s,dy)\,ds,\quad t\in[0,T].

Proof.

By using the mild formulation we get

\displaystyle\langle\rho(t),\phi(t)\rangle-\langle\rho_{0},\phi(0)\rangle
